INTRODUCTION 

This Commentary analyzes the traffic ordinances of the 100 largest cities in the United States. It will compare those ordinances with the Model Traffic Ordinance, and with some provisions of the Uniform Vehicle Code, especially those which were formerly part of the Ordinance. It will identify and describe provisions found in the 

100 city ordinances which are not part of either the Model Traffic 

Ordinance or the Uniform Vehicle Code. The Commentary will broadly assess the scope of traffic regulation in the 100 cities and the authority for such regulation, and it will narrowly discuss the substance of individual sections commonly found in the 100 ordinances. 

In general, the purpose is to present a comprehensive picture of the status of municipal traffic regulation in the United States. 

The Model Traffic Ordinance is a specimen set of traffic ordinances for a municipality and is consistent with the recommended state law embodied in the Uniform Vehicle Code.1 The provisions of the Ordinance are designed as a guide or standard for municipalities to follow in reviewing or adopting their own traffic ordinance. 

The entire text of the current Model Traffic Ordinance is included in an Appendix to this Commentary. 

The Uniform Vehicle Code is a specimen set of laws designed and recommended as a comprehensive standard for state motor vehicle and traffic laws.2/ The Code is not based on theory, but on actual experience under various state laws throughout the nation. It reflects the need for uniformity in traffic regulation throughout the United States and, to this end, serves as a reliable, contemporary guide for use by state legislatures.

Both the Model Traffic Ordinance and the Uniform Vehicle Code are maintained by the National Committee on Uniform Traffic Laws and Ordinances, an independent, non-profit, voluntary association. 

The Committee is a carefully selected group of approximately 140 representatives of federal, state and local governments, insurance companies, motor clubs, safety groups, manufacturers, dealers, trade associations, unions, national transportation associations and other individuals and groups interested in achieving sound, uniform motor vehicle and traffic laws and ordinances. 

The 100 cities selected for this study are those with the 3 largest populations as identified by the 1970 U.S. Census.. 

Throughout the Commentary, the cities will be cited in the order shown below. They are arranged alphabetically by state names, and within a single state, alphabetically by city names. The purpose of this arrangement is to make intrastate similarities and differences more readily apparent. The 100 cities are as follows:

2 -

These These 100 cities include at least one city from 36 of the 50 4 states. Twenty-six of the states are represented by more than one city, including California with 11, Texas with eight, and Ohio with seven. Fourteen states have no cities included in the study.5/

The ordinances used in this study are those which were provided by various city officials in response to our requests.6/ 

We requested a copy of all traffic ordinances, updated through 

December 31, 1976. Except as specifically noted below, we received a set of traffic regulations, generally in some codified form and appearing to be reasonably complete and up-to-date, from each of the cities. Since we had no practical way to verify that the ordinances were complete and up-to-date, we made no attempt to do so, but used whatever we received. An Appendix to this 

Commentary includes a citation or description of the ordinance or other material used in this study for each of the 100 cities. 

We received some response from each of the 100 cities, but the following problems should be noted: 

One city, Gary, Indiana, was completely unable to send us any traffic ordinances, even 14 months after our initial request. 

Officials in Gary explained that they were in the process of revising the traffic code, and that revisions in state laws were delaying the project.

One other city, Mobile, Alabama, was also in the process of revising its ordinances. Mobile officials provided a set of traffic provisions which appear to be part of the proposed revision. It is unclear whether the regulatory material provided to us had actually been adopted, or was just pending. fied.

The ordinances provided by two cities were entirely uncodi- 

Paterson, New Jersey sent several hundred individual, uncodified, ordinances, some well over 50 years old. Very little of the substance of these ordinances proved to be useful to the study; most of them specified rules applicable only to a particular, named street or location within the city. Lubbock, Texas sent copies of 49 separate, uncodified ordinances, some over 30 years old.

These ordinances, although difficult to work with in uncodified form, did state general rules and were considerably more useful in the study than those of Paterson. - 3 - 

---

Providence, Rhode Island apparently gives the traffic engineer authority to make regulations covering the operation of vehicles. Accordingly, Providence forwarded no ordinances, but sent nine uncodified administrative regulations established by the Traffic Engineering Department. These regulations were appropriate to the content of this study. 

In spite of these problems, all 100 cities were retained in the study. The Providence administrative regulations and the 

Mobile proposed ordinances are given the same treatment as the ordinances of the other cities. In every analysis of a particular substantive provision, Gary is shown as having no comparable provision. Paterson is almost always in the same category.

The primary purpose of the Commentary is to broadly assess the status of municipal traffic regulation in the United States, rather than to report that status as to any particular cities. 

For that reason, and to avoid filling the text with often repeated lists of city names, the names of particular cities being discussed are not identified in the text of the Commentary. They are always identified by footnote, however. The Commentary uses two types of footnotes for this purpose. Tabular footnotes, referenced by two letters (AA/, e.g.), are used to identify cities making up large groups. Regular footnotes, referenced by numerals (1/, e.g.), are used to identify the cities making up smaller groups, or for other footnoted information. 

The first portion of this Commentary contains a discussion of the authority of the cities to adopt and enforce the ordinances. The Commentary then analyzes the 100 ordinances in greater detail. This is done in the context of the Model Traffic 

Ordinance. The Model Traffic Ordinance is divided into 22 

Articles, each relating to a different aspect of traffic regulation. The Commentary proceeds through these Articles in order, one at a time. The substance of each Article is described and the extent of adoption by the cities, of the whole Article, or various important concepts of the Article, is then discussed. 

Following this, each section of the Article is described individually, and all comparable sections found in the 100 city ordinances are discussed. After this section-by-section analysis, the 

Commentary describes any provisions found in the 100 municipal ordinances which relate to the subject of the Article being discussed but which have no direct counterpart in the Model Traffic Ordinance. Frequently these provisions have counterparts in the - 4 - 

---

Uniform Vehicle Code, and this is noted in the discussion.

The 

Commentary then proceeds to the next Model Traffic Ordinance 

Article and reviews it in the same manner. After all of the 22 

Articles have been discussed, the analysis concludes by describing those provisions found in the 100 ordinances which are not relevant to the subject matter of any of the 22 Articles of the 

Model Traffic Ordinance. A summary and the author's observations regarding the status of municipal traffic regulation follow the analysis.

AUTHORITY FOR TRAFFIC ORDINANCES Statutory Authority Uniform Vehicle Code. The authority of municipalities_to adopt and enforce traffic ordinances comes from state laws.7/ 

The extent and nature of the authority controls the scope of the municipal ordinances.

The Uniform Vehicle Code specifies that the state vehicle code provisions are applicable throughout the state and in all political subdivisions and municipalities. Under the Code, local authorities are specifically prohibited from adopting or enforcing any ordinance covering a subject matter which is covered by the state law (the Uniform Vehicle Code), unless such an ordinance is expressly authorized by the state law. The Code section provides as follows: 

The provisions of this act shall be applicable and uniform throughout this State and in all political subdivisions and municipalities therein and no local authority shall enact or enforce any ordinance on a matter covered by the provisions of this act unless expressly authorized. 8

Another section of the Uniform Vehicle Code specifically enumerates and limits the powers of local authorities.9 

The effect of these Code provisions in any state adopting them is that local ordinances may not conflict with, nullify, supersede or otherwise alter the substance of a state law unless specifically allowed to do so by the state law. Nor are the municipalities allowed to enact or enforce ordinances which duplicate the state law provisions. Such duplication is unnecessary since the local authorities can and should enforce the appropriate state law. - 5 -

It

Consistent with this grant of authority in the Uniform Vehicle Code, the Model Traffic Ordinance is limited in scope. It contains no provisions which duplicate Uniform Vehicle Code provisions. contains no provisions which conflict with the Uniform Vehicle Code, except as expressly authorized.10 It complements the Uniform 

Vehicle Code, providing for application of regulations at particular locations and covering regulatory needs which are unique to municipalities and not covered by the Uniform Vehicle Code. 

Prior to 1968, the Uniform Vehicle Code provisions on powers of local governments were quite different. At that time, the Code contemplated that municipalities would enact and enforce ordinances which duplicated state laws on the same subject. 

The Code pro hibited ordinances in conflict with the state law, but did not prohibit ordinances on the same subject matter if they were consistent with the state law.11 At that time, the Model Traffic 

Ordinance contained many sections which merely duplicated sections in the Uniform Vehicle Code. All of these provisions were deleted from the Model Traffic Ordinance in 1968 when the Uniform Vehicle 

Code provisions on powers of local governments were revised. 

The National Committee made these revisions in 1968 after recognizing that municipal ordinances which duplicated state laws were not only unnecessary, but ultimately always resulted in nonuniformity when the municipalities failed to keep up with changes in the state laws. For example, the state law and municipal ordinance describing a driver's duty at a red light might start out the same, but when the state amended its law to allow a right turn on red and the city failed to make the same change, the law and ordinance would then conflict.

The primary reason for maintaining duplicatory municipal ordinances appears to be the allocation of fines and forfeitures, the city getting the money in cases where local ordinances are violated, and the state getting it in cases where the violation of a state law is charged. This is a poor reason to require every city to maintain many ordinances which merely duplicate the state laws. The state could simply enact a law which allocates revenue from fines and forfeitures on some equitable basis other than whether the violation charged is a state law or a city ordinance. The Uniform 

Vehicle Code provision allocates the revenue to the state, county, city or town which maintains the court wherein the conviction was had.12 - 6 -

Two approaches. Thus, in terms of authority for municipal traffic ordinances, and the resulting scope of those ordinances, we can identify two basic approaches. Under the first approach, the one currently used by the Uniform Vehicle Code and Model Traffic Ordinance, municipal ordinances complement but do not duplicate the state laws. State laws are enforced within the municipality by the local police and courts. Local ordinances cover only those matters not regulated by the state law. Under the second approach, the one used by the Uniform Vehicle Code and Model Traffic Ordinance prior to 1968, municipal ordinances are much more extensive, generally duplicating the provisions of the state law. Local police and courts enforce the local ordinance within the municipality, rather than enforcing the state law. 

Study cities. Eight of the 100 study cities are located in four states (Alabama, Arizona, Kentucky and Massachusetts) which do not have a provision in the state vehicle code which defines the authority of local jurisdictions to adopt and enforce traffic ordinances. 

One more of the 100 study cities (Washington, D.C.) is not located within a state, and its authority to enact and enforce traffic laws is very similar to the authority of the states, rather than that of the other study cities.

The other 91 of the 100 study cities are located in states having vehicle code provisions which incorporate one or the other of the two approaches to municipal authority described above. 

Thirty of these 91 cities are located in eight states which follow the first approach.AA/ Seven of these states (California, Florida, Georgia, Hawaii, Maryland, New Jersey and Pennsylvania) have laws similar to the Uniform Vehicle Code provision prohibiting local ordinances on any matter covered in the state code. The eighth state (New York) does not prohibit ordinances on the same subject matter but does prohibit any local ordinances which "duplicate" a state law, except as specifically allowed. 

Of these 30 cities, the ordinances of 21 appear to be completely in compliance with the statutory grant of authority. These 

21 cities do not have ordinances which duplicate or otherwise cover the same subject matter as a state law, except as specifically authorized.AB

Four more of the 30 cities have ordinances which comply substantially with the statutory grant of authority; the ordinances contain few provisions which duplicate state laws.14/ - 7 - 

---

The other five cities, all from Florida and Georgia, have extensive 15/ ordinance provisions duplicating state laws. 

One of the Florida cities, at least, is aware of the problem. Tampa, Fla. officials who sent the ordinances for the study had circled each of the sections which they consider valid and enforceable. They excluded the sections which would be nullified by the Florida law. 

The other 61 of the 91 cities are located in 24 states which follow the second approach. AC/ These 24 states have laws which prohibit municipal ordinances which conflict with the state law, but they allow municipalities to have ordinances covering the same subject as a state law, so long as the ordinance is consistent with the law. The 24 states Colorado Connecticut Illinois Indiana Iowa Kansas Louisiana Michigan Minnesota are: 

New Mexico 

North Carolina Ohio Mississippi Missouri Nebraska Oklahoma Oregon

Rhode Island 

Tennessee Texas Utah Virginia Washington Wisconsin

We would expect that a complete, section-by-section comparison of the ordinances in these 61 cities with the state law provisions they are intended to duplicate would yield many differences and quite a few conflicts. Since some revisions are made every year in the state vehicle codes, the cities must be very careful to regularly amend their ordinances to reflect the revisions in the state laws. If they fail to do so, differences and conflicts will result.

We have not attempted a complete, section-by-section comparison of all the ordinances with their state law counterparts. But in order to approximate the extent of the problem, we have compared several ordinance provisions from each of the 61 cities with the appropriate state law counterpart. We have assessed the following three points of comparison: 

1. Where should a motorist facing a steady red signal light stop? The Uniform Vehicle Code provides that a driver must stop at one of three points: a stop line if there is one; before entering a crosswalk if there is no stop line; or before entering the 16 intersection if there is neither a stop line nor a crosswalk.

The law in the state of Washington, for example, conforms with the Uniform Vehicle Code in this respect, but the city ordinances of both Spokane and Tacoma list only two stopping points: before - 8 - 

---

the crosswalk or before entering the intersection.17/ This is a subtle difference between the state law and the municipal ordinances, but under certain circumstances it might be construed to constitute a conflict. 

2. Is a right turn on red permitted?

The Uniform Vehicle 

Code and the laws of most states, including Michigan, allow right 18/ turns after stopping, unless a sign prohibits the turn.

The city ordinances of Flint, and Warren, Mich., however, allow a right turn on red only if a sign at the intersection permits the turn. This is a very clear conflict between the state law and the municipal ordinances. 

3. What is the meaning of a steady yellow signal light displayed following a green light? Under the Uniform Vehicle Code and most state laws, a driver facing a yellow light may enter and clear the intersection.19/ Some states, however, provide that a driver facing a yellow light following a green light may enter the intersection but may not be crossing it when the red light is shown. 

Several states follow a third interpretation of a yellow light which requires drivers to stop before entering the crosswalk or intersection unless such stop cannot be made in safety. The law in the state of Missouri conforms with the Uniform Vehicle Code in this respect, but the ordinance of  requires a driver to stop for a yellow light unless the stop cannot be made This is another example of a direct conflict bewith safety.20/ tween the state law and municipal ordinance. 

For all of the 61 cities in this category, we have compared the ordinances and state laws on these three points. The analysis reveals a substantial amount of conflict between ordinances and state laws in states where such conflict is prohibited. 

Of the 61 cities located in states which prohibit conflicting local ordinances, only 26 have no conflicts in the above three areas AD The municipal ordinances of 35 cities contain at least one inconsistency or possible conflict with the state vehicle code. 

The described stopping points in 29 of these cities differ from AE the stopping points in their respective state codes. 

The municipal ordinances of 21 of the 35 cities do not conform to the right turn on red provision found in the state codes. AF The municipal ordinances of 13 of the 35 cities are inconsistent with the state AG provisions on the meaning of a steady yellow light.

- 9 -

Home Rule 

Quite a few states have authorized counties, cities, or towns to adopt "home rule" charters which eliminate, to some extent, the authority of the state legislature over the municipality, and give the municipality full power of local self government as to all subjects which are strictly of municipal concern and not in conflict with the constitution or general laws.21 

In most states this would not affect the authority for traffic ordinances as described above, since the regulation of traffic is considered to be a state affair. Generally, the state motor vehicle code supersedes and prevails over home rule charters and ordinances in conflict therewith.22/ It

There are some exceptions to this general rule, however. appears, for example, for example, that home rule jurisdictions in the state of Colorado are authorized to adopt and maintain traffic ordinances which directly conflict with some state traffic laws, although they lack that authority as to some other state traffic laws. 

Contrary to judicial decisions in most states, Colorado courts have stated that, in general, the regulation of traffic in a home rule jurisdiction is a matter of local concern and that local ordinances supersede any conflicting state statutes. 

23/ Courts have held that the following issues are matters of local concern: regulation of traffic at street intersections; speed limits; right-ofway; parking; careless driving; reckless driving; stops at flashing red school bus lights; and designation of one-way streets. But the state has the right to regulate the following matters: driving while intoxicated; driving while a person's license is suspended; requiring a driver involved in an accident to stop, render aid and report the accident. Non-home rule cities in Colorado, however, may not enact or enforce motor vehicle ordinances superseding state 24/ statutes.

An extensive review of the complex matter of home rule authority in each of the states is beyond the scope of this Commentary, but the reader should be aware that a home rule charter may, in some cases, affect the authority of the home rule jurisdiction to enact and enforce ordinances which differ from the state law on the same subject. 

10 -

Article II 

Traffic Administration Overview of the Article Article Two of the Model Traffic Ordinance deals with the administration of the city traffic program. It divides responsibility for the traffic program between a traffic division established in the police department, and the office of the city traffic engineer. The traffic division is responsible for investigation and enforcement activities, while the traffic engineer is responsible for management of traffic operations.108/ Coordination of the overall program is in the hands of a traffic commission, a group of city officials including the traffic engi- 109/ neer and the head of the police traffic division. 

Thirty-two of the 100 cities generally follow the adminis trative approach of the Model Traffic Ordinance. DO 

Twenty of these 32, however, omit the section providing for a traffic commission.DR/

Eleven of the 32 omit the section which creates the traffic division within the police department, but these 11 cities assign the duties of the traffic division to the police department in general.DS

Article Two of the Model Traffic Ordinance also requires the traffic division to maintain records of traffic related incidents, 110/ including violations by type, accidents by location, and drivers by name.

The driver's file must include all accidents, warnings, arrests, convictions and complaints appropriate to the driver.111/

These records are used for traffic safety activities. 

Annually, the division must prepare a report concerning traffic matters in the city to be submitted to the mayor.112/ 

Eighteen cities have all of these record-keeping provisions.DT/ 

Nine other cities omit only one of the requirements cities, however, have none of these requirements.

DU Fifty-nine

The Model Traffic Ordinance Article also requires the traffic division to investigate accidents and to cooperate with the traffic engineer in conducting studies and developing remedial measures for dangerous locations.114/ 

20 -

Twenty-one cities have adopted all of the three Ordinance sections which impose these duties.DV/ Sixty cities, however, have adopted none of the three sections. DW Finally, the Article authorizes the police chief, with the approval of the traffic engineer, to make regulations necessary to carry out the city traffic ordinances. 

Temporary regulations to cover emergencies are also authorized. 1157 authorized.1167

Fifty-one cities have adopted a similar ordinance authorizing regulations.117

On the whole, Article Two has not been widely adopted by the 

100 cities. Only four cities have something comparable to each of the 12 sections.118/ 

Another eight cities lack only one of the sections, most omitting MTO § 2-12 which establishes the traffic commission.119/ Another 22 cities have something comparable to at least half of the Model Traffic Ordinance sections.DX Twenty one cities, however, have nothing comparable to any of the sections from Article Two relating to administration of the city traffic program.DY/ 

The least adopted provision in the Article is MTO § 2-12 

establishing a traffic commission. Although the traffic commission concept has been in the Model Traffic Ordinance since its inception in 1928, only 20 of the 100 cities have a comparable provision.120,

Section Analysis 

This section of the 

MTO § 2-1 -- Police administration. 

Model Traffic Ordinance establishes a traffic division within the police department. The ordinances of 27 cities establish a traffic division in the police department.DZ/ The ordinances of the other 73 cities do not provide for such a division. MTOS 2-2 

Duty of traffic division. The Ordinance specifies the duties of the traffic division, including enforcement of the traffic regulations, accident investigation, and cooperation with other agencies to improve traffic conditions. 

The ordinances of 41 cities have a provision comparable to MTO § 2-2, although many of these ordinances refer to the duties of the police department in general, rather, than to the duties of a traffic division of the department. The remaining 59 cities have no comparable ordinance. EA 

- 21 -

All of the 41 comparable ordinances specify that the duties include enforcement of the traffic regulations Thirty-eight of these specificallý refer to enforcement of state traffic laws as well as city traffic regulations.122/ 

Of the 41 comparable ordinances, 35 list accident investigation as a duty. EB Thirty-three of the comparable ordinances require the traffic division, or the police department in general, to work to improve traffic conditions.EC/ 

MTO § 2-3 Records of traffic violations. The Model Traffic 

Ordinance requires the police to keep a public record of all traffic violations and their final dispositions for a five-year period. In 

30 cities, the ordinances have some similar record-keeping provision.ED/

Five of these 30 specify a different retention period for the records. 123, The remaining 70 cities have no similar provision. 

MTOS 2-4 Traffic division to investigate accidents. This section of the Ordinance requires the traffic division to investigate accidents, make arrests, and assist in prosecution of law violators who cause or contribute to accidents.

Twenty-seven city codes have a provision comparable to this section.EE 

Of these 27 cities, three only have the investigation requirement.124/ 

The other 73 cities have no comparable provision.

MTO $ 2-5 -- Traffic accident studies. The Model Traffic 

Ordinance requires the traffic division to cooperate with the city traffic engineer in conducting studies and developing remedial measures for locations where accidents are numerous. 

The traffic regulations of 30 municipalities have a similar provision. EF

The other 70 city codes contain no similar provision. 

MTO $ 2-6 -- Traffic accident reports. The Ordinance requires the traffic division to maintain an accident-report-filing system, and to make it available to the city traffic engineer. 

Thirty-seven city ordinances have a provision comparable to 

MTO § 2-6.ÉG/ The remaining 63 municipalities have no comparable provision. Nine of the 37 comparable ordinances make the reports confidential; they are generally available only to city officials for traffic safety purposes. 

1257 Four of the nine also make the reports privileged; they are generally not admissible in civil or criminal proceedings.126/ 

22

-- MTOS 2-7 Drivers' files to be maintained. This section of the Model Traffic Ordinance requires the police department to keep an alphabetical record of drivers, including all accidents, warnings, arrests, convictions and complaints for each driver for a five-year period. The division is also required to study the cases of drivers involved in frequent or serious law violations or accidents, and report to the appropriate state agencies.

MTO S 2-10 City traffic engineer. The Model Traffic Ordi nance provides for appointment of a city traffic engineer under civil service rules, or alternately makes the city engineer the traffic engineer in addition to his other functions. In either case, he must be a qualified engineer. The traffic engineer's duties include installing and maintaining traffic-control devices, analyzing traffic accidents and devising remedial measures, ducting traffic engineering investigations, planning traffic operations, and developing means to improve traffic conditions. EL Fifty-three city ordinances contain similar provision, although many specify additional duties. Nine of the 53 designate the city engineer as the traffic engineer, in addition to his other functions. 129/ Eleven of the 53 have no provisions regarding appointment of the traffic engineer; they merely specify his duties. 130/

MTOS 2-11 Emergency and experimental regulations. This section empowers the police chief, with the approval of the city traffic engineer, to make regulations necessary to carry out the traffic ordinances, and to make temporary or experimental regulations, effective for up to 90 days, to cover emergencies or special conditions. The section also authorizes the traffic engineer to test traffic-control devices under actual traffic conditions. 

1971 amendment authorizes the police chief to place temporary traffic-control devices when required by an emergency, provided that he notifies the traffic engineer as soon soon as practicable.

EM/

MTO S 2-12 11 

Traffic commission --powers and duties. The 

Ordinance establishes a traffic commission and defines its membership and duties. The commission is made up of a small number of city officials who are directly involved with traffic administration, including the chief of the traffic division and the city traffic engineer. The function of the commission is to coordinate the official traffic activities of the city government. 

A footnote to the Ordinance suggests that a city might also use a larger commission, with more citizen members and more public officials, and with additional responsibilities to coordinate a community traffic safety education program. 

Twenty city ordinances contain a similar provision. EN/ Of the 20, all but two utilize the alternate, larger commission mem- 134/ bership suggested by the footnote.. 

Fourteen of the 20 cities provide that both the traffic engineer and the police chief or head of the traffic division are members or participants in commission activities, and one city provides that only the engineer is a member. 135/ All but one of the 20 cities make it a function - 24 

---

of the traffic commission to coordinate city traffic safety activities.136/

Nine of the ordinances provide that the commission is to be in charge of a traffic safety education program.. 137/ 

Article III 

Enforcement and Obedience to Traffic Regulations

Overview of the Article Article Three of the Model Traffic Ordinance deals primarily with enforcement of the traffic laws applicable within the city. 

Responsibility for enforcement is given to the police department.138/ 

Police officers are authorized to direct traffic as necessary, and officers of the fire department are also authorized to do so at the scene of a fire.139/ 

Seventy-six of the 100 cities have an ordinance comparable to this MTO provision dealing with the duty to 140/ enforce traffic laws and authority to direct traffic. These provisions are discussed in detail in the Section Analysis for 

MTO $ 3-1, infra. nance.

Article Three also requires obedience to traffic laws. One section generally requires obedience to the city traffic ordi- 141 Other sections define more specifically the kind of obedience which is required for certain classes of traffic; specifically, persons propelling push carts or riding or driving animals or animal-drawn vehicles on the roadway, persons on skates or toy vehicles public employees, and drivers of authorized emergency vehicles.142 These provisions have been widely adopted by the 100 cities. Thirty of the cities have all five of these Model 

Traffic Ordinance provisions.EO/ Another 27 have all but one of the provisions.EP Surprisingly, the section which generally requires obedience to the traffic ordinance is the most often omitted provision in these 27 cities. Fourteen more cities have three of the five provisions.143/ Again, the general provision is the most frequently omitted. Only 12 cities have none of these Model Traffic 

Ordinance provisions.144

The remaining two sections of Article Three deal with a somewhat different subject. They require written accident reports to 145 be made to the police as to accidents occurring in the city. 

These are the least adopted provisions of the Article. Only 11 cities have both sections.

EO Another 22 have one of the sections.ER/ 

The remaining 67 cities have neither section. 

- 25 ·

Article III of the Model Traffic Ordinance has a relatively high rate of adoption. Only six cities have all eight of the sections.146/ Another eight cities have adopted all but one of the sections.147/ But quite a few cities have adopted most of the sections. A total of 60 have more than half of the sec- 148 tions.ES 

Only nine cities have none of the sections.

The adoption picture looks even better when the two sections relating to accident reports are omitted from the analysis. Considering only the enforcement provisions, 26 cities have adopted all six sections, ET and another 28 omit only one section.EU A total of 70 cities have more than half of the enforcement provisions.EV

Section Analysis MTO S3-1 -- Authority of police and fire department officials. 

This section provides that it is the duty of officers of the police department to enforce the city traffic ordinances and the state vehicle laws. Such officers are authorized to direct traffic by voice, hand or signal. Traffic direction must generally be in conformance with traffic laws, but in the event of a fire or other emergency or to expedite traffic or safeguard pedestrians, police officers are authorized to direct traffic as conditions require, notwithstanding any provisions of the traffic law. At the scene of a fire, officers of the fire department may direct traffic, or assist the police in so doing. 

Seventy-six cities have a comparable section, although some EW of them do not have all of the provisions of MTO § 3-1. other 24 cities have no comparable section.

MTO § 3-6 Public employees to obey traffic regulations. 

This section makes it clear that the traffic ordinance applies to all drivers regardless of the fact that the vehicle being driven is owned or operated by a government agency. Sixty-seven cities have a similar ordinance.FJ

MTOS 3-7 Authorized emergency vehicles. This section provides that the driver of an authorized emergency vehicle, when operating under defined emergency conditions, may park the vehicle without regard to provisions of the ordinance which would otherwise prohibit such parking, but the driver must use due care to protect the safety of others.

Prior to its amendment in 1968, this section also exempted authorized emergency vehicle drivers from ordinance provisions requiring drivers to stop at red lights and stop signs, and to obey speed limits and regulations governing direction of movement and turning. These exemptions applied only when the vehicle was using the required red lights and siren.155/ Another section which required the drivers of other vehicles to pull over to the right and yield upon the approach of an authorized emergency vehicle was also deleted in 1968.156/ Since these provisions are found 157/ in the Uniform Vehicle Code, they are not necessary in the MTO. 

Many of the city ordinances discussed below retain them, however.

Written report of accident.

This section requires the driver of a vehicle involved in an accident resulting in death, injury or serious property damage to make a written report to the police unless the police investigated the accident at the scene while the driver was there. Article IV 

-1 

Traffic-control Devices Overview of the Article 

One important function of this Article is to provide for the installation and the legality of official traffic-control devices. 

Sections require installation of devices needed to give effect to the ordinances, authorize installation of other devices needed to regulate, warn or guide traffic, specify when the devices are required for enforcement purposes and create a presumption that the 167 devices are official and legal.. 

Many cities have adopted similar provisions, although only nine have all four of the sections described above.168/ Twentyfive cities have three of the four provisions, most commonly omitting the section which creates a presumption of the legality of the devices.FS/ Forty-four cities have one or two of the provisions.FT/ Twenty-two cities have not adopted any of the sections, relating to installation or legality of traffic-control devices. FU The other provisions in Article IV generally relate to special traffic-control areas which are created by installation of trafficcontrol devices by the engineer. These sections relate to play streets, crosswalks, safety zones, and traffic lanes.169 

Thirteen cities have all four of these provisions. FV/ Thirtynine additional cities have the provisions on crosswalks, safety zones and traffic lanes, but omit one or both of the play street sections.FW/ Thirty-two cities have adopted none of the provisions relating to special traffic-control areas. FX Most cities have some provisions relating to traffic-control devices. Only three cities have a section comparable to each of the eight sections in Article IV. 

170/ Another nine omit only one section, generally the one which creates a presumption of legality for the devices.171 

Thirty-eight_additional cities have at least half of the Article IV provisions.FY/ 

The Model Traffic Ordinance formerly included a number of sections describing the meaning of official traffic-control devices for drivers and pedestrians. These sections were deleted in 1968 since they are adequately covered by the Uniform Vehicle Code. 

Well over half of the cities have most of these provisions in their ordinances. The Model Traffic Ordinance also formerly included sections to prohibit unauthorized devices and to prohibit any 

30

interference with official devices. Two-thirds of the cities retain both of these provisions. All of these provisions are discussed in detail at the end of the Section Analysis. 

Section Analysis 

MTO S 4-1 

Authority to install traffic-control devices.

This section requires the traffic engineer to place and maintain official traffic-control devices as necessary to give effect to the city traffic ordinances. The engineer is authorized to place and maintain additional devices as he deems necessary to regulate, warn or guide traffic. 

Sixty-six cities have a comparable provision. FZ/ Eight of these 66 have only the requirement and not the additional authorization to install devices.172/

Six of the cities require an engineering and traffic investigation before installing a device. 

The remaining 34 cities have no comparable provision. 173/ 

MTO S 4-2 Manual and specifications for traffic-control devices. This section requires all traffic signs, signals and devices to conform to the manual and specifications approved by the state highway commission (or alternately, a manual approved by a city-adopted resolution). Signs and signals for a particular purpose are to be uniform by type and location. All devices so erected and not in conflict with state law or city ordinance are official devices.

A comparable provision on device specifications and manual is in the ordinances of 43 cities.GA/ 

Five of the 43 cities refer to the Manual on Uniform Traffic Control Devices instead of, or in addition to, a state approved manual, and one other city specifies that traffic-control devices must conform to American Association of State Highway and Transportation Officials standards.174 One other municipality specifies device types and locations instead of referring to a manual.175/ The other 57 cities have no comparable provision.

Article V Speed Regulations Overview of the Article 

This Article provides for regulation of speed within the municipality. The general approach is that the state speed limit laws are applicable within the municipality except where the city traffic engineer has placed signs to change the limit. The engineer is given authority in specifically described circumstances to change the state speed limits. 197/ Only three cities follow the approach described above. 

Another 17 cities generally make state speed limits applicable, but they allow variation of those limits only by the city council.198 

Sixty-five of the 100 cities have city speed limits, rather 199 than making the state limits applicable.

The limits are generally all specified in the ordinance. Some of these ordinances specify a single, city-wide speed limit. 

Some others specify 

- 35

several limits, applicable to different kinds of streets or in different zones or districts. Some others contain extensive schedules giving the speed limit individually for every street in the city. Twenty-three of these cities authorize administrative variations in the city speed limits.. 200/

Almost half of the 100 cities have a basic speed rule in their ordinances. 2017 Thirty-five cities, have some limits which 202/ 

Fourteen of these 35 are prima facie rather than absolute. cities with prima facie speed limits are not among the cities which have the basic speed rule, however. 203 

Fifteen of the 100 cities have no provisions on speed. 204/ Generally, Article V is one of the least adopted of the 

Model Traffic Ordinance Articles. Our analysis indicates most cities have rejected the basic Model Traffic Ordinance approach to speed regulation.

Section Analysis 

MTO $ 5-1 -- State speed laws applicable. This section provides that the speed limit specified in the state traffic law applies on all city streets except where the city traffic engineer has determined a different limit based on an engineering and traffic investigation, and has placed signs giving notice of the limit. 205 Any such limit is an absolute limit; it is unlawful to exceed it.

Prior to its amendment in 1971, this section required any alterations of the state speed limits to be made by the governing body of the city, rather than by the traffic engineer, and to be 206/ declared in the municipal ordinances. 

Twenty cities follow the basic approach of the Model Traffic

Ordinance to speed regulation.GT Ordinances of these cities spe cify in some way that the state speed limits generally apply except as altered by local authorities at particular locations. Three of the 20 cities have a provision similar to current MTO § 5-1.207/

All three authorize alteration of the state limits by an administrative agency, although none of the three gives that authority to the city traffic engineer. The other 17 of the 20 cities use an approach which is similar to the former Model Traffic Ordinance section. All 17 provide for alterations of state speed limits at particular locations only by the governing body of the city. Generally, these alterations are listed in schedules within the 

36-

ordinance itself. 

All 20 cities require an engineering and traffic investigation prior to alteration of any speed limits. 

The ordinances of another 65 cities use a different approach GU to speed regulation. Instead of making the state speed limits generally applicable, these ordinances specify city speed limits. 

Fifty-eight of these 65 ordinances specify generally applicable city speed limits and then alter the limit at particular locations by action of the governing body of the city or by an administrative agency. Thirty-one of these 58 specify a single city-wide speed limit.GV The other 27 specify several limits applicable on different kinds of highways within the city. GW 

Twenty-eight of the 58 provide for alteration of the city speed limits only by the governing body of the city.GX/ Sixteen of these 28 specifically require an engineering and traffic investigation.GY/

Twenty-two of the 58 provide for alteration of the limits by an administrative agency.

GZ/ 

Ten of these 22 specifically require an engineering and traffic investigation. ordinances provide for alteration of the limits primarily by a 2087 state administrative agency, at the request of the city. remaining two of the 58 have only the city-wide limits; there is 209 no specific provision for alteration of these limits..

The other seven of the 65 cities establish city limits which are applicable at particular locations within the city. In six of these cities, all limits are set out in extensive schedules 210/ contained in the ordinances. 

In the one other city, all limits are established by an administrative agency for particular locations.211

The Model Traffic Ordinance provides that speed limits within the city are absolute limits; it is unlawful to exceed such limits. 

Of the 85 cities which have ordinances relating to speed limits, HC 50 provide for absolute limits. The other 35 provide that some or all limits are prima facie rather than absolute limits. 

MTO S 5-2 Authority of city traffic engineer. This section authorizes the city traffic engineer to exercise the power given to 212/ local authorities to alter state speed limits. Specifically, he may decrease limits at intersections and outside urban districts, increase limits within urban districts, and establish limits on - 37

arterial streets. He may do so only on the basis of an engineering and traffic investigation, and consistent with restrictions imposed by the state law. The limits set by the engineer may apply at all times or at particular times as specified by official traffic control devices. This section was added to the 

Model Traffic Ordinance in 1971. 

Prior to 1971, this authority was exercised through ordinance by the governing body of the local government. 

At that time, the Model Traffic Ordinance included four additional sections which were to be used by municipalities to make changes in state speed limits at particular locations. The sections provided for decreasing state limits at specified intersections, increasing the state limits in specified zones, decreasing the state maximum limit on specified streets, and establishing speed limits on specified arterial streets.214

These sections were deleted in 1971, when authority to alter the state limits was given to the city traffic engineer.

None of the 100 cities has an ordinance conforming to current MTO S 5-2. However, 26 cities do have ordinances which broadly authorize an administrative agency to establish speed limits or to vary limits established in some other way. HE/ Although none of these 26 ordinances conforms to the current Model Traffic Ordinance provision, they are somewhat similar because the authority is given to the traffic engineer or some other administrative agency.

HF Forty-five other cities have ordinances which allow variations in otherwise applicable state or city speed limits to be made only by the governing body of the city. Many of these 45 use ordinances conforming to the four former Model Traffic Ordinance sections in order to set out the variations. 

The remaining 29 cities have no ordinance comparable to 

MTO § 5-2.HG 

MTO § 5-3 Ambulance drivers to comply with speed limits.

The section, which was added to the Model Traffic Ordinance in 1975, requires the driver of an ambulance to comply with maximum speed limits in the city, notwithstanding the state law exempting authorized emergency vehicle drivers from such compliance.215

The Uniform Vehicle Code contains specific authority for this ordinance.216/ 

- 38 -

Phoenix, Ariz. specifies that a "private emergency vehicle," whether or not proceeding under an emergency situation, may not exceed the speed limit. Wichita, Kans. provides that an ambulance may not exceed the speed limit by more than 15 mph. 

The other 98 cities have no comparable provision. As already noted in the Section Analysis of MTO § 3-7, most of the 100 

cities have an ordinance exempting authorized emergency vehicles from compliance with the speed limits under certain circumstances. MTO S 5-4 MTO S 5-5 MTOS 5-6

Article VI 

Turning Movements

Overview of the Article Article Six of the Model Traffic Ordinance deals with turning movements. The first two sections provide authority for the city traffic engineer to place official traffic-control devices in order to regulate the course of turning vehicles, or to prohibit or restrict turns at particular intersections. The last section in the Article restricts U-turns. 

Twenty-three cities have all three provisions. HJ/ Another 

38 have two of the three provisions.HK Fourteen of those 38 have

HL/ the first two sections dealing with authority of the engineer. 

Fifteen cities have none of the Article Six provisions.!

HM - 39 -

218/ Sixty cities have a provision restricting U-turns 

The Section Analysis for MTO § 6-4 describes a substantial degree of nonuniformity among these ordinances. One problem relates to 

U-turns at intersections. Quite a few cities prohibit U-turns at intersections, especially signalized intersections. But some other cities specify intersections as the only lawful place to make a U-turn.

The Model Traffic Ordinance formerly included sections describing the required course for turning vehicles, and specifically requiring obedience to no-turn signs.219 A total of 70 cities still have one or both of these provisions which are generally covered by state law. 

Section Analysis MTO S 6-1 

HN Authority to place devices altering normal course for turns. The course normally to be followed by a vehi- 220 cle making a turn is specified in the Uniform Vehicle Code.. 

MTO § 6-1 authorizes the city traffic engineer to erect official traffic control devices indicating the course to be traveled by turning vehicles, and that course may conform with or differ from that specified by the state law. 221/ 

Formerly the Model Traffic Ordinance, as well as the Uniform Vehicle Code, contained a section specifying the normal course for turning vehicles. 222/ The section was deleted in 1968 when all duplicating provisions were removed from the Model Traffic Ordinance. 11

Article VII 

One-way Streets and Alleys

Overview of the Article 

Article Seven gives the traffic engineer authority to establish one-way streets, and streets with reversible lane flow, by installing traffic-control devices.

As the Section Analysis indicates, most cities retain the former Model Traffic Ordinance approach to one-way streets, providing for their designation by the city council rather than by the traffic engineer.255 Less than one-fourth of the cities have a provision authorizing reversible lane flow installations.. 256/ 

Section Analysis 

MTO S 7-1 -- Authority to sign one-way streets and alleys.

This section authorizes the city traffic engineer to designate one-way streets and alleys by placing appropriate official traffic control devices.

Prior to its amendment in 1971, this section required oneway streets and alleys to be designated by the governing body of the city in the ordinance. The traffic engineer was required to place official traffic control devices on streets and alleys so designated. The section specifically required signs indicating the lawful direction of traffic movement to be placed at every intersection.257

Two cities have provisions similar to the current Model 

Traffic Ordinance section.258/ Another 53 cities have a provision similar to the former Model Traffic Ordinance section.

Article VIII 

Stop and Yield Intersections Overview of the Article 

This Article has been reduced to one section authorizing the city traffic engineer to regulate traffic at intersections by means of traffic control devices. As described in the Section 

Analysis, most cities still leave this authority with the city council rather than the traffic engineer. 

This Article is an excellent example of the difference in scope between the current and former Model Traffic Ordinances. 

The current Ordinance contains only the one section relating to the traffic engineer's authority. The former Ordinance contained four sections relating to authority; it also contained six sections describing the duties of drivers at stop and yield intersections, all but one of them duplicating provisions in the Uniform 

Vehicle Code. 

Thirty cities have at least three of the four former Ordinance provisions dealing with authority.ID/ On the other hand, 27 cities have none of those provisions.IE/

Twenty-one cities have all six of the former Ordinance provisions dealing with drivers' duties at intersections 

34 cities have at least four of those six sections.IG cities have none of the six provisions.IH/ IF

Section Analysis 

MTO S 8-1 Authority for stop and yield signs. This section authorizes the traffic engineer to erect stop signs, yield signs, or other traffic control devices designating through streets or intersections and roadway junctions where traffic on one or more of the roadways should yield, or stop and yield. The section was added to the Model Traffic Ordinance in 1971, consolidating several then-existing sections and expanding the authority of the traffic 262/ engineer with regard to through streets..

Although no city has a provision conforming to this MTO section, five cities give the traffic engineer comparably broad authority to designate through streets and stop or yield intersections. 263/ Two other cities allow the engineer to designate through streets..

Designation of through streets.

provided that streets named in a schedule which was part of the ordinance were designated as through streets. Thirty-six cities have a comparable provision under which the governing body of the city, rather than the traffic engineer, designates through streets.II

Signs required at through streets. Prior to 1971, the

Model Traffic Ordinance provided that whenever an ordinance of the city designated a through street, it was the duty of the traffic engineer to place a stop sign or yield sign on every street intersecting the through street, unless the intersection was controlled by signals. Where two through streets intersected, the engineer could sign either one, on the basis of a traffic and engineering survey..

Other Related Provisions 

Duty of Drivers 

Duty to stop at stop or yield signs. Prior to 1968, the 

Model Traffic Ordinance required a driver approaching a yield sign to stop, if required for safety, and described the position where the stop should be made. The section further required a driver approaching a stop sign to stop unless directed to proceed by a police officer or traffic signal, and described the position where the stop should be made.2697 These provisions were deleted from the Model Traffic Ordinance because they are covered by the 

Uniform Vehicle Code.. 270/ 

Thirty-eight cities still have comparable provisions. IM/ - 46 -

Duty to yield at stop intersections. Prior to 1968, the 

Model Traffic Ordinance required a driver facing a stop sign, after having stopped, to yield to a vehicle approaching on the intersecting highway so closely as to constitute a hazard. 271/ 

This requirement is covered by the Uniform Vehicle Code. 272/ 

Sixty-two cities have a similar provision.IN

Duty to yield at yield intersections. Prior to 1968, the 

Model Traffic Ordinance required a driver facing a yield sign to yield to a vehicle approaching on the intersecting highway so closely as to constitute a hazard. If a collision resulted when a driver passed a yield sign without stopping, the Ordinance section provided that this was prima facie evidence of his failure to yield.273/

This provision is covered by the Uniform Vehicle Code.274

Fifty-three cities have retained all or part of this provision. IO/ 

Duty on emerging from a driveway. Prior to 1968, the MTO required the driver of a vehicle emerging from an alley, driveway or building within a business or residence district to stop and yield to pedestrians before crossing the sidewalk, and to yield to vehicles before entering the roadway.275, This requirement, in greatly modified form, is in the Uniform Vehicle Code. 

Seventy-five cities have a similar provision.IP/ 276/ Duty regarding train signal. Prior to 1968, the Model Traffic 

Ordinance required a driver to stop in response to described signals indicating the approach of a train.277/ The Uniform Vehicle 

Code covers this requirement. 

278/ Forty-five cities have a similar provision.IQ/

Duty to stop for obstructed traffic. Prior to 1971, the 

Model Traffic Ordinance contained a section which prohibited entering an intersection or marked crosswalk unless there was sufficient space on the other side to accommodate the vehicle, without blocking the intersection or crosswalk.279/ This section was moved from the Model Traffic Ordinance to the Uniform Vehicle 

Code in 1971.280/ 

Seventy-one cities have a similar ordinance.IR

Article Xx 

Streetcars

Overview of the Article 

The emphasis of this Article is the regulation of streetcar operation. The first two sections regulate boarding, alighting, and riding on streetcars and vehicles. Since they have some application beyond streetcars, these sections have been retained by a reasonably large number of cities. The third section authorizes the traffic engineer to designate streetcar stops. cities have this section and most that do refer to bus stops rather than streetcar stops. Few

Obviously, there is little interest in streetcar regulation in a city which has no operating streetcar system.

Article XII 

Regulations for Bicycles

Overview of the Article 

This Article provides special rules for bicycles. Most of the sections effectuate a registration requirement. In addition to the registration provisions, the Article includes sections specifying the effect of the bicycle regulations and a penalty for their violation; a section providing that bicyclists have the same rights and duties as the drivers of other vehicles; and a section allowing bicycles to be parked by attaching them to certain publicly owned poles. Formerly, the Article included a large number of rules for operating a bicycle. They were deleted, generally because they duplicated Uniform Vehicle Code provisions. 

Most of the cities have some special provisions applicable to bicycles. Fifty-one cities have ten or more such provisions.KM/ 

Twenty-three cities, on the other hand, have no such provisions. KN ко Another 17 have less than five special bicycle provisions. Some cities may have bicycle regulations in a separate code and may have neglected to send them for inclusion in the study.

Although only 36 cities require bicycle registration and three others have voluntary registration programs, a total of 41 cities have provisions comparable to one or more of the Model Traffic Ordinance sections on registration.. 332/ Thirty cities have at least half of the nine registration provisions of the Model Traffic Ordi- 

KP/ Eleven cities have fewer than half.KQ 

The other 59 nance. cities have no bicycle registration sections.

Regarding the other current Model Traffic Ordinance sections, those not dealing with bicycle registration, 20 cities have more than half of the provisions.KR Another 34 cities have fewer than half of the provisions.KS The other 46 cities have no comparable sections.

Concerning the 16 rules for operating bicycles which were formerly in the Model Traffic Ordinance, 34 cities have ten or more of these rules.KT Another 21 cities have at least four but less than ten of these rules. KU Nineteen cities have at least one but less than four of the rules.KV/ In ten of these 19 cities, the only bicycle rule is the one which prohibits clinging to another vehicle.333/

The remaining 26 cities have no provisions comparable to the 16 bicycle operating rules formerly found in the 

Model Traffic Ordinance.

MTO S 12 Attaching bicycle to poles. This section allows attaching a bicycle to any public pole for up to 12 consecutive hours, unless a sign or another law or ordinance prohibits such parking at that location. Bicycles may not be attached to any tree, fire hydrant, or police or fire call box, and may not be secured so as to obstruct the normal movement of pedestrians or other traffic. Of the 100 cities, only the District of Columbia has a comparable provision. 

MTO $ 12-15 Penalties. This section defines special penalties for violation of the bicycle provisions. The penalties are a fine and/or temporary impoundment of the bicycle or its registration plates. Twenty-four cities have a comparable penalty provision.LK/ 

The subject of bicycle impounding as a penalty, and also with regard to abandoned or unregistered bicycles, is discussed in another part of the Commentary.345/

Riding on roadways and bicycle paths. This former Model 

Traffic Ordinance section contained three subsections. The first required a person riding a bicycle on a roadway to ride as near to the right side of the roadway as practicable. The second subsection prohibited riding more than two abreast, except on paths or parts of roadways set aside for exclusive use of bicycles. 

The third subsection required use when available of a usable, adjacent bicycle path, instead of riding in the roadway.363/ 

These provisions were deleted from the Model Traffic Ordinance because they duplicated provisions in the Uniform Vehicle Code.364, Forty-one cities have a provision similar to the first subsection, requiring bicyclists to stay as close as practicable to the LX/ right side of the roadway. 

Thirty-three cities have a provision like the second subsection generally prohibiting riding more than two abreast.LY/ Six other cities prohibit riding two abreast; they require bicycles on a roadway to always ride single file. 365/ Four other cities require riding single file in certain described circumstances.. 366/ Thirty cities have a provision like the third_subsect ection re- quiring use of a bike path adjacent to a roadway.

Lamps and equipment. The Model Traffic Ordinance formerly contained three subsections dealing with bicycle equipment. The first required bicycles in use at night to be equipped with a white light visible 500 feet to the front, and a red reflector visible 300 feet to the rear when illuminated by upper beam headlights. A red light visible 500 feet to the rear could be used in addition to the reflector, but was not required. The second subsection required a bicycle to be equipped with a bell or other device audible from 100 feet, but sirens and whistles were prohibited. The third subsection required a bicycle to have a brake capable of making the braked wheel skid on dry, level, clean pavement.367 367 This section was deleted from the Model Traffic Ordinance in 1968 because it duplicated requirements in the Uniform Vehicle Code 368 Forty-nine cities have a provision similar to the first subsection, requiring a headlight and red rear reflector on bicycles 60 -

used at night.MA/ Four of these 49 all in Ohio, require a taillight in addition to the reflector.369/ One other city allows the bicyclist to wear a light visible to the front in lieu of having a headlight on the bicycle.370/ Another city requires a reflector on the front, but not a headlight.371/

Thirty-five cities have the second subsection requiring a bell or other audible device and prohibiting sirens and whistles on bicycles.MB

Forty-three cities have a brake requirement similar to the third subsection of the former Model Traffic Ordinance provision.MC Article XIII

This Article now contains three sections, all relating to angle parking. Formerly, the Article dealt with the method of parking a vehicle, in general. The other section required parking lights on vehicles parking on dark roadways or shoulders.

Article XIV 

MM 

Stopping, Standing or Parking

Prohibited in Specified Places Overview of the Article 

This Article regulates the parking of vehicles on the roadway. 

Formerly the Article contained an extensive section which prohibited parking at specified locations, standing at other specified locations, and stopping at a third group of specified locations. Although this section was deleted from the Model Traffic Ordinance in 

1968, 80 cities still have a similar provision.383/

Of the nine sections which remain in the Article, six prohibit, or authorize prohibition of, only parking. The other three also authorize prohibition of standing; and one section also authorizes a prohibition of stopping.

The first four sections of the Article prohibit parking under specified circumstances. Eighteen cities have all four of these sections MN/ 

Another 61 cities have at least two of the four sections. 

MO Only five cities have none of these parking prohibitions.384/

The next five sections of the Article all authorize the city traffic engineer to erect signs in order to regulate parking, and to a lesser extent, standing and stopping. These five sections have not been as widely adopted as the first four. Fourteen cities have all five of the sections.MP/ But only 17 other cities have at least three of the five sections.MQ 

Forty-one cities have none of these provisions. MR 63

MTOS 15-1 -- City traffic engineer to designate loading zones. 

This section authorizes the traffic engineer to determine the location of loading zones and passenger loading zones, and to place signs marking them and stating the applicable laws. 

Sixty-four cities have a provision similar to this section. NP/ 

Four of the 64 only specify freight zones; two others specify only passenger zones; one allows passenger zones only at hotels, and 404/ two allow such zones only in business districts 

MTO S 15-2 Permits for loading zones and passenger loading zones. The Ordinance provides that the traffic engineer may not designate a loading or passenger loading zone upon request unless the person applies for an annual permit, and for two signs to mark the zone. The engineer must collect a fee for the permit and signs, and may impose use and return conditions, and require reimbursement if the signs are lost or damaged.

Only 27 cities have a similar section.NO/ There are many variations in the working of these ordinances, such as charging by the lineal foot or exempting city or federal government agencies from the fee, for example. The remaining 73 cities have no such provision.

Stopping, standing and parking of buses and taxicabs regulated. This section has four parts. Article XVI 

NZ 

Eight of 

Stopping, Standing and Parking Restrictions

Overview of the Article 

By amendment in 1971, this Article was reduced to one section which gives the city traffic engineer broad authority to regulate stopping, standing, and parking by means of traffic-control devices. 

No city has a similar section.

Prior to the 1971 amendments, the Model Traffic Ordinance con tained provisions for regulation of stopping, standing, and parking by the governing body of the city. Seventy-six cities have one or more of the seven former Model Traffic Ordinance sections.OA/ Thirtyfour of these 76 have four or more of the seven sections.OB 

Section Analysis MTOS 16-1 -- Authority of city traffic engineer. This section gives the engineer broad authority, on the basis of an engineering and traffic investigation, to prohibit, regulate, or limit the stopping, standing, or parking of vehicles. No such prohibition, regulation, or limitation is effective unless indicated by official trafficcontrol devices, and it is the duty of the traffic engineer to erect and maintain such devices.

None of the study cities has an ordinance giving the city traffic engineer comparably broad authority to regulate stopping, standing, and parking.

Prior to 1971, the Model Traffic Ordinance provided for regulation of stopping, standing, and parking at particular locations by the city council or other governing body of the city, instead of by 

69

the city traffic engineer. At that time, Article 16 contained seven sections to implement that policy. Many cities still have provisions comparable to those seven sections, as described below. 

Application of article. This section specified that the standing and parking prohibitions of Article 16 applied at all times, or at those times specified in the Article or on the sign.413/ Thirtysix cities have a comparable provision.OC

Regulations not exclusive. This section specified that the restrictions in Article 16 did not relieve any person from the duty to observe other and more restrictive provisions prohibiting or limiting vehicle stopping, standing, or parking. 414 Thirty-five cities have a comparable provision.OD 

Restrictions on scheduled streets. Four former Model Traffic

Ordinance sections specified restrictions applicable when signs were erected to give notice on streets or areas listed in schedules which were part of the ordinance. The first section prohibited parking at any time on streets specified in a schedule.415/ second section prohibited parking during hours and on streets or in 

The districts specified in a schedule, except on Sundays and holidays.416/ 

The third section was like the second, but included stopping and standing as well as parking.417/ The fourth section prohibited parking more than a specified number of hours during certain hours of the day on streets or in districts specified in a schedule, except on 

Sundays and holidays.418,

A number of cities have similar sections, although many of them omit any reference to schedules, and no schedules of parking restrictions accompanied their ordinances. It is possible in these cities which omit the schedules that the traffic engineer has authority to determine where these restrictions apply by erecting appropriate signs, but the authority is not clear from the ordinance. Fifty-one ОЕ cities have a provision like the first section described above, but 30 of the 51 omit the schedules.OF Thirty-nine cities have a section similar to the second section described above, OG/ but 20 of these omit the schedules.OH Thirty-five cities have a provision like the third section,OI/ but 18 omit the schedules. OJ Fifty-two cities have a section comparable to the fourth section described above, OK but 30 of the 52 omit any reference to schedules.OL/

Altogether, 64 cities have one or more of the four sections.OM 

Only 26 of the 64 refer to schedules or include them with their ordinances.ON/ 

The other 38 do not. - 70 -

Article XVII 

Regulating Classes and Kinds of Traffic on Certain Highways Overview of the Article 

This Article authorizes the city traffic engineer, on the basis of engineering and traffic investigations, to erect trafficcontrol devices in order to exclude certain classes or kinds of traffic from streets or parts of streets. Traffic can be excluded on the basis of excessive weight or due to vehicle size or because it is incompatible with other traffic. 

Only ten cities have provisions similar to any of the four Model Traffic Ordinance sections, and none of the ten has all four sections. One city has three of the sections and three cities have two; the other six each have only one section. 421/ 

Prior to amendment in 1971, this Article of the Model Traffic 

Ordinance provided for exclusion of certain kinds of traffic from certain streets only by the city council. Three of those former sections still appear in many city ordinances. Nine cities have all three.422 tions.OP

Another 35 cities have at least one.00/

Size restrictions.

traffic on that street. Only three cities have a similar section, and one of the three applies it only to controlled-access highways.429/

Article XIX 

Penalties and Procedures on Arrest Overview of the Article 

Most of the sections which made up this Article were deleted from the Model Traffic Ordinance in 1971. The Article now contains only five sections.

The first section is the general penalty provision applicable to all violations under the Model Traffic Ordinance. Two-thirds of 435/ the study cities have similar provisions..

Two more of the five sections deal with the disposition of fine revenue. Only one-fourth of the cities have comparable provisions.436/

The last two sections of the Article deal with impoundment of vehicles. Ninety cities have some provisions relating to vehicle impoundment, although they vary widely.437/ 

Twenty-four cities have sections comparable to half or more of the Model Traffic Ordinance provisions which were deleted in 

1971.PA 

Another 53 cities have some of these sections but fewer than half. PB/

Five of the twelve deleted sections related to forms, record- 438 keeping, and controls for traffic citations. 

Five cities have all five of these sections.439/ Another 12 cities have three or four of the sections. PC/ Twenty-five cities have one or two. 

The other 58 cities have nothing comparable. PD Four of the deleted sections related to enforcement of traffic 441/ laws.440/
