Section Analysis MTOS 19-1-- Penalties. This is the general penalty provision applicable to all offenses under the Model Traffic Ordinance unless another penalty is expressly provided by law. For a first or second offense the penalty is a fine of not more than dollars. The adopting city is to fill in an appropriate maximum fine. For a third or subsequent offense within a one-year period, the penalty is a fine of not more than dollars, or imprisonment for not more than days, or both. Again, the adopting city is to insert appropriate maximums for the fine and term of imprisonment. PJ/ PK/ Sixty-five cities have a comparable general penalty provision.PI 

Twenty-three of these specify a fine as the only penalty option. 

Thirty-seven others provide for a fine and/or term of imprisonment. 

The other five cities have sections which refer to penalty specifications in a part of the city code which was not provided for purposes of this study. 443/

MTO SS 19-2 through 19-10 MTO S 19-11 

[Sections deleted in 1971]. 

Disposition of traffic fines and forfeitures. 

This section requires all fines and forfeitures assessed for violations of the ordinance to be placed in the highway improvement fund of the city treasury. This fund is to be used only for construction, maintenance, or repair of streets and traffic-control devices. 

Twenty-four cities have a similar section.PL/ Twelve of these 

24 omit the specific restrictions on use of the fund, however.PM/ 

Nine more of the 24 cities specify that the fines and forfeitures PN/ are to go to the general fund. One city specifies that fines and forfeitures for speeding violations go to the general fund, while those for other violations go to a highway improvement fund.

The other two cities with comparable provisions simply provide for disposition according to state law.445 444/ 

MTO S 19-12 -- Official misconduct. This section specifies that failure to comply with the requirements relating to disposition of fines and forfeitures constitutes official misconduct and grounds for removal from office. Only four cities have similar provisions.446/

MTO S 19-13 

Impounding vehicles. Subsection (a) specifies that a police officer may remove a vehicle from a highway to a garage or other place of safety whenever authorized by state law. 

Uniform Vehicle Code (the state law referenced) authorizes removal of abandoned vehicles.447/

It further authorizes removal of The 

75 -

unattended vehicles which are obstructing traffic on any highway.448/ 

It also authorizes police officers to remove any vehicle found on the highway which has been reported stolen, or when the person in charge of the vehicle has been arrested or is otherwise unable to provide for removal of the vehicle.449/ Prior to 1968, the Model 

Traffic Ordinance section specified the circumstances under which a vehicle could be impounded rather than referring to state law for that authority. That provision allowed removal of an unattended vehicle obstructing traffic on any bridge or in a tunnel, a disabled vehicle obstructing traffic on a highway when the person in charge is unable to remove it because of physical injury, and a vehicle which is illegally parked so as to obstruct traffic on a highway.450/ 

Subsections (b) and (c) relate to requirements for notification of the owner of an impounded vehicle. When the name and address of the owner can be determined from the registration or from records in the vehicle, subsection (b) requires the police officer to give immediate written notice to the owner of the fact of the removal, the reason for it, and the storage location of the vehicle. If the vehicle is stored in a public garage, a copy of the notice must be sent to the garage proprietor. When the officer is unable to ascertain the name of the owner or is for any reason unable to give the required notice, and if the vehicle is not returned to its owner within a period of three days, subsection (c) requires the officer to send a written report to the state agency responsible for motor vehicle registration. That notice must contain a complete description of the vehicle, the date, time, and place of removal, and the storage location. If the vehicle is stored in a public garage, a copy of the report must be sent to the proprietor of the garage. 

Ninety cities have ordinances comparable to MTO § 19-13 au- PO thorizing impoundment of vehicles under specified circumstances. 

These ordinances vary substantially from the Model Traffic Ordinance provision and from one another, however. 

Only one of the 90 cities has a provision similar to subsec tion (a) of the current Model Traffic Ordinance section. San Jose, 

Cal. authorizes a police officer to impound a vehicle whenever authorized by state law. The other 89 cities, like the former 

Model Traffic Ordinance section, specify particular circumstances under which a vehicle may be impounded, rather than relying upon state law for authority to impound.

Fifty cities have some provision to authorize impounding to prevent obstruction of traffic.PP/ - 76 -

Forty-one of these 50 cities authorize impounding any vehicle which is unattended upon a highway in a position so as to obstruct or endanger traffic flow. PQ/ Seventeen of the 41 additionally require that the obstructing vehicle be in violation of a parking standing, or stopping ordinance in order to justify its removal.PR 

Twenty-six of the 50 cities have the former Model Traffic

Ordinance provision authorizing removal of an unattended vehicle obstructing traffic on any bridge, tunnel, or similar structure. PS/ 

Four of those 26 do not specifically require that a vehicle left on such a structure be creating an obstruction in order to remove it.451

One of the 26 allows removal only if the vehicle is illegally standing or parked.452/

Twelve of the 50 cities have provisions which allow impoundment of vehicles which are obstructing traffic at particular locations such as a driveway entrance. PT A total of 71 cities have some provision to allow impounding a vehicle which is illegally parked.PU/ 

Fifty of these 71 cities authorize impoundment of any unattended vehicle on a highway which is in violation of any stopping, PV/ standing, or parking ordinance. Two more are nearly as broad but specifically omit overtime parking less than 12 hours as a basis for 453/ impounding.- Eleven more of the 71 specify particular violations for which a vehicle may be impounded.. 

Thirteen of the 71 cities specifically authorize impoundment of vehicles parked in signed or marked towaway zones. PX/ Twenty-one cities have a provision to authorize impounding a vehicle which has been reported stolen.. PY Twenty-eight cities authorize impounding a vehicle when the driver or other person in charge of the vehicle has been arrested. PZ/ 

A total of 40 cities have a provision to authorize impounding vehicles which have been involved in an accident, but these provisions differ significantly as described below.QA 

Nineteen of the 40 resemble the former Model Traffic Ordinance provision which authorized impounding any vehicle on the highway when it is so disabled as to constitute an obstruction to traffic and the person in charge of it is unable to provide for its custody because of physical injuries.QB/ - 77

Ten more of the 40 cities have a provision which refers only to the condition of the vehicle; it must be disabled and obstructing traffic but the person in charge need not be physically inca- 454/ pacitated in order for the vehicle to be impounded.. 

Four more of the 40 cities have provisions which refer only to the physical condition of the person in charge of the vehicle; if he is injured the vehicle may be impounded without regard to the condition of the vehicle itself.455/ 

The other seven of the 40 cities authorize impounding the vehicle either because the person in charge is injured and unable to provide for it, or because the vehicle is disabled and obstructing traffic.. 456/

Thirty-eight cities have ordinances which provide for impoundment of a vehicle left unattended on a street continuously for more than a specified period of time.QC The most commonly specified time periods are 72 hours (14 cities) and 48 hours (nine cities), although the range is from 24 hours (five cities) to ten days (two cities). While many of these ordinances allow impoundment on the basis of the time period alone, some are related to provisions regarding abandoned vehicles. In these latter cases, the time period usually serves as the basis of a presumption that the vehicle is abandoned.

Forty-two cities have ordinances which provide for impoundment of an abandoned vehicle left on a street.QD Many of these ordinances do not adequately define what constitutes an "abandoned vehicle" for purposes of impoundment. Of those which do impose some standards to define abandonment, continuous parking without movement for more than a specified time period is the most common criterion. Twenty of the 42 cities use that standard.QE

Twenty-nine cities have provision for removal of abandoned vehicles from private property and other nonhighway areas.QF 

Twenty-eight cities have ordinances which allow impoundment of vehicles for public safety or convenience during emergencies.QG 

Most of these ordinances cover snow emergencies although some cover fires, floods and other emergency conditions.

Seventeen cities have ordinances which allow impoundment of vehicles which would constitute an obstruction to street cleaning, snow removal, or other highway maintenance.QH 

78 -

Fourteen cities provide for impoundment of a vehicle if it is on the highway, and not in proper operating condition as required by law.QI Nine cities provide for impoundment of a vehicle if it is on the highway and it does not have current valid state vehicle registration.457/

Fourteen cities have ordinances which include additional mis- 458 cellaneous grounds for impounding a vehicle on a highway Most of the vehicle impoundment provisions discussed above authorize impounding simply because the vehicle meets the described criteria. Some of the cities add an additional element to the law; their ordinances declare the offending vehicle to constitute a public nuisance, subject to summary abatement. This approach is very commonly used in the ordinances relating to vehicles abandoned or in wrecked condition on private property. 

459/ But 13 cities use this approach, in some or all situations, for impounding vehicles left on the highway.QJ Several other cities have provisions in their impoundment laws which are apparently intended to insure the legitimacy of the impoundment.460

Regarding the duty of the police officer to give notice of the impoundment to the vehicle owner, 44 of the 90 cities have a provision comparable to subsections (b) and (c) of the Model Traffic Ordinance section. QK/ Many of these 44, like the Model Traffic Ordinance, specify different procedures for owner-ascertainable, owner-nonascertainable cases. and 

Seventy-one of the 90 cities have a provision dealing with redemption of the impounded vehicle by the owner on payment of towing and storage fees.QL/ 

Twenty-nine cities have provisions relative to the sale of unclaimed, impounded vehicles.

allows impounding or immobilizing any vehicle found on a street if the vehicle is registered in the name of a scofflaw. A person who has failed to satisfy five or more parking or traffic citations and for whom an arrest warrant has been issued is a scofflaw.

---

Traffic citation forms. Prior to 1971, the Model Traffic 

Ordinance included a requirement that the (treasurer) of the city provide books of serially-numbered blank traffic citation forms to the police, and that he obtain a receipt and keep a record of all such books issued. The citations were to be in quadruplicate, and in form jointly prescribed and approved by the chief of police and the (chief city magistrate). The chief of police was responsible for distribution of the books to individual officers.462/ 

Uniform Vehicle Code has a similar provision relating to control of citation books.463/ 

Thirty-five cities have a provision comparable to this former Model Traffic Ordinance section.20

The 

Disposition and records of citations. Prior to 1968, the 

Model Traffic Ordinance contained an extensive section specifying procedures to be followed after issuance of a citation.464 

The procedures related to proper disposition of the charge and requirements for recordkeeping. Subsection (a) specified the proper distribution of the four copies of the citation. Subsection (b) provided that upon filing of the citation with the traffic court, the charge could only be disposed of by official action of the court. 

Subsection (c) required the return of any citation forms not issued to violators but which have been marked upon or spoiled to the chief of police. Subsections (d) and (e) required the police chief to keep records of all citations issued, the disposition of the charge, and any warrants issued. Subsection (f) made it unlawful for any officer or public employee to dispose of any citations or records except as required by the section. The Uniform Vehicle Code contains a similar section.465/

Twenty-five cities have one or more of the provisions from this former Model Traffic Ordinance section.QP/ 

Only seven of the 25 have all or nearly all of the subsections, however.466 

The other 18 have only one or two of the subsections. 

Subsection (a) is the most widely adopted of the provisions; 16 of the cities have it although many of them vary the distribution of the copies of the citation. 

Illegal cancellation of traffic citations. The 1962 Model 

Traffic Ordinance also contained a provision specifically making it unlawful for any person to cancel or to solicit the cancellation of any traffic citation, except as provided by ordinance. 467/ 

Fifteen cities have a comparable provision.QR 

Audit and reports.

Prior to 1971, the Model Traffic Ordinance required a periodic audit by the city (treasurer) of all records of citations, complaints, and warrants. A public report of the audit 

80

was to be submitted to the mayor and city council. The (treasurer) was specifically given access to all records necessary for such an audit. Another subsection required a periodic report to be published in a local newspaper to summarize all citations issued and their disposition. 

468 The Uniform Vehicle Code has a similar provision regarding a periodic audit and report. 469/ 

Only 17 cities have an audit requirement comparable to part of this former MTO section.QS

Only two of those 17 also have the subsection requiring newspaper publication of a report of citations issued and their disposition.470/

Traffic court records and reports. The 1962 Model Traffic 

Ordinance also contained an extensive provision requiring traffic courts to keep records of all complaints, citations and charges, and all official action regarding them, and to report all convictions other than those for illegal standing or parking to the state department of motor vehicles within 10 days 4717 The Uniform

Vehicle Code has a similar section.472/ Fourteen cities have a section like this former MTO section.QT/ 

Procedure of police officers. Prior to 1971, the Model Traffic 

Ordinance contained a section requiring a police officer to issue a traffic citation to a person halted for a traffic violation, unless the person is taken immediately before a magistrate as authorized or required by state law, or unless the person is given only a warning. Upon issuing a citation, the officer was required to take relevant identifying information, and upon receiving written promise of the person to appear to answer the charge, to release the person from custody.4737 The Uniform Vehicle Code has a similar provision relating to issuance of citations.474/ Forty-four cities have a provision comparable to this former Model Traffic Ordinance section.QU Traffic citation as a lawful complaint. The 1962 Model Traffic 

Ordinance contained a section providing that a traffic citation in proper form and sworn to as required by law constitutes, a lawful complaint when filed with a court having jurisdiction.475 

Only nine cities have a similar section.476/ 

Violation of promise to appear. Prior to 1971, the Model Traffic

Ordinance contained a section making it unlawful to violate a written promise to appear given to an officer upon the issuance of a traffic citation.477 The Uniform Vehicle Code has the same provision.478/ 

Thirty-one cities have a similar provision.QV

- 81 -

When warrant to be issued. Prior to 1971, the Model Traffic 

Ordinance contained a section specifically providing for issuance of an arrest warrant upon failure of a person to respond as required to a citation.480 

Twenty-eight cities have a comparable section.QW

Citation on parked vehicle. Prior to 1971, the Model Traffic 

Ordinance specifically authorized an officer to affix a traffic citation to a vehicle when the vehicle is illegally stopping, standing, or parking, and the driver is not present.481/ The section was moved to the Uniform Vehicle Code in 1971.482/ Forty-one cities have a comparable section. QX

Failure to comply with citation on parked vehicle. A corollary section which was also moved from the Model Traffic Ordinance to the Uniform Vehicle Code in 1971 provided that if the violator failed to respond to the citation affixed to a parked vehicle within five days, the court or violations bureau was required to send a notice to the registered owner of the vehicle to inform him of the violation and warn that failure to respond within five days would result in issuance of an arrest warrant.483, Twenty-nine cities have a comparable section.QY 

Presumption as to illegal parking. A third section relating to citations for illegal parking was also moved from the Model 

Traffic Ordinance to the Uniform Vehicle Code in 1971.5 484/ This section created a prima facie presumption that the registered owner of a vehicle was the person who illegally parked it. Fifty-two 

cities have a comparable provision.

Article XX 

Effect of and Short Title of Ordinance Overview of the Article 

This Article has nine sections providing a general legal framework within which the other provisions of the Model Traffic Ordinance operate. It is by far the least well adopted Article of the Model 

Traffic Ordinance. Five of the nine sections have been adopted by ten or fewer cities.485/

None of the sections has been adopted by more than 36 cities.

Mobile, Ala. has all nine of the Article's sections. Knoxville, Tenn. has eight of the nine, and Honolulu, Ha. has six. Three cities,

Denver, Colo.; and Spokane, and Tacoma, Wash., each have five of the sections. Twelve cities have three or four of the sections. RA 

82

Forty cities have only one or two of the nine sections from the 

Article.RB 

The remaining 42 cities do not have any provisions like those found in this Article of the Model Traffic Ordinance. 

Section Analysis 

MTOS 20-1 

Application. This section specifies that the ordinance provisions apply only on the highways, unless a different place is specifically referred to in a given section. Twentyseven cities have a comparable provision. RC Eight of these 27 extend application of the ordinance provisions to private roads 486/ and parking lots which are open to public vehicular travel.

MTOS 20-2 Uniformity of interpretation. This section specifies that the ordinance is to be interpreted and construed so as to effectuate its purpose to make local ordinances uniform. 

Only five cities have a comparable provision.:

MTOS 20-3 

487/ Effect of headings. This section provides that article and section headings shall not be deemed to govern, limit, modify or in any way affect the scope, meaning or extent of any article or section provision. Only four municipalities have a provision resembling this section.488/

MTOS 20-4 Short title. The section specifies that the ordinance may be known and cited as the (city name) (Model) Traffic 

Ordinance. There are 36 cities with a provision comparable to the ordinance.RD

MTOS 20-5 

Ordinance not retroactive. This section provides that the ordinance is not retroactive and does not apply to a traffic accident, cause of action arising out of an accident or judgment arising therefrom, nor to any violation of the motor vehicle ordinance of the city which occurred prior to the effective date of the present ordinance. Only three of the 100 cities have a similar section.489/

MTOS 20-6 

Effect of partial invalidity. The ordinance provides that if any part or parts of it are held invalid for any reason the decision will not affect the validity of the remaining portions of the ordinance. Twenty-four cities have a comparable provision.RE - 83 

---

MTOS 20 

Publication of ordinance. This section requires the city clerk to certify passage of the ordinance and publish it in some way. Several different publication options are provided in the section; the adopting municipality is to select one and make it part of their ordinance. The options are newspaper publication of the whole ordinance, newspaper publication of as much of the ordinance as required to comply with the state constitution and laws, or newspaper publication of notice of passage along with a summary of the provisions and copies of the whole ordinance made available for public inspection. visi 4907 Only six cities have a comparable provision located in their motor vehicle and traffic ordinance. 

MTO § 20-8 

Repeal. The section repeals existing, inconsistent provisions. Twenty-two cities have comparable provisions relating to repeal (or nonrepeal) or prior ordinances.RF MTO $ 20-9 

Effective date. The section specifies the effective date of the ordinance. Ten cities have a similar provision.491/

Article XXI 

Schedule of Designated Streets Referred to in Ordinance [Article deleted in 1971] 

Prior to 1971, this Article of the Ordinance contained schedules (lists) of particular streets or portions of streets where other provisions of the Ordinance were made applicable. This included provisions such as one-way streets, through streets, parking restrictions, load limits, etc. City ordinances which use schedules to apply substantive provisions at particular locations are discussed under the appropriate substantive provision.

Article XXII (Appendix) 

Parking Meter Zones Overview of the Article 

This Article, which is actually an Appendix to the Model Traffic Ordinance, provides for the use of parking meters in designated zones within the city.

Although the provisions vary in some respects from the Model 

Traffic Ordinance and from each other, all but four cities have ordinances which provide for the use of parking meters.492/ Most of these cities have a provision comparable to all or most of the 

Model Traffic Ordinance sections. Thirty-five cities have sections comparable to all seven of the Model Traffic Ordinance sections. RG/ 

Thirty-four more have provisions like six of the seven sections. RH 84 

---

Section Analysis 

MTOS 22-1 Parking meter zones. The section authorizes the traffic engineer to establish parking meter zones on those streets or parts of streets where necessary to regulate parking. 

The meters are effective between hours specified by the engineer on all days except Sundays and legal holidays. 

Prior to amendment in 1971, this Model Traffic Ordinance sec tion specified that parking meter zones were established by the city council and were designated in a schedule which was part of the ordinance. The traffic engineer was authorized to establish additional zones,, but these were subject to the approval of the city council.493/

RI/ 

Sixty cities have some provision comparable to MTO § 22-1¸RI 

Only seven of the 60 are similar to the current Model Traffic 

Ordinance section, however.494/

Thirty-one more of the 60 are similar to the former Model 

Traffic Ordinance provision.RJ Twenty-one of these 31 omit part of the former provision, however. 495/ The remaining 22 of the 60 cities have miscellaneous provisions which relate to establishing parking meter zones but they differ from the Model Traffic Ordinance section.RK 

MTOS 22-2 Installation of parking meters. The section requires the traffic engineer to install meters in the designated meter zones. It describes the location of the meter and manner of operation, including a requirement that the meter display the amount of parking time remaining and indicate by appropriate signal when time has expired. The meter must also bear a legend indicating applicable parking restrictions. The section also specifies the ratio of parking time to coins deposited, with a footnote indicating that the amount charged should be adjusted to reflect current practice in the municipality. cities have comparable provisions. 

RL Eighty-four

MTO S 22-3 Parking meter spaces. This section requires the engineer to designate by roadway and/or curb markings a parking space adjacent to each meter. It requires a parked vehicle to be within the lines designating the space, although a very large vehicle is permitted to occupy two spaces as long as both meters are paid. Seventy-eight cities have comparable provisions. RM/ - 85 - 

---

MTOS 22 

Deposit of coins and time limits. The section prohibits parking in a meter space at a time when the meter requirements apply without depositing appropriate coins. The section allows using the unexpired time left on the meter by another parked vehicle, however. It prohibits parking in the space while the meter shows that parking time has expired, except for the time necessary to park and insert the coins. It also prohibits parking in a meter space for a longer consecutive period than the maximum permitted in that zone, regardless of the number of coins put in the meter. Ninety-two cities have a comparable provision. RN/ MTOS 22-5 

Use of slugs prohibited. The section prohibits use of slugs or other devices instead of U.S. coins in a parking meter. Seventy-nine cities have a similar section.RO 

MTOS 22-6 

Tampering with meter. The section prohibits tampering with any parking meter. Eighty-three cities have similar sections.RP

MTOS 22-7 Application of proceeds. This section provides for collection and use of parking meter revenues. Such revenue is kept in a special fund used for purchase, installation and maintenance of parking meters, and for expenses in operation of the meter system and enforcement of parking meter restrictions. Net proceeds after expenses are used for parking studies, and for expenses in providing public off-street parking facilities. Seventy-three cities have a comparable section.RO/ 

OTHER ORDINANCE PROVISIONS Uniform Vehicle Code Provisions 

496

Many cities have ordinance provisions which are comparable to sections of the Uniform Vehicle Code. 

Some of these provisions have already been discussed because they were formerly also in the Model Traffic Ordinance or because they relate closely to the con tent of one of the Articles of the Model Traffic Ordinance. 

In this portion of the Commentary we will outline the Uniform Vehicle Code, indicating very briefly the number of comparable ordinances we have located in the 100 cities, and where appropriate, providing reference to the prior portion of the Commentary (Comparison of Model Traffic Ordinance and City Ordinances) where those ordi nances are discussed in greater detail.

Chapter 

Words and Phrases Defined 

These definitions are discussed under the Comparison of MTO 

Article I with the city ordinances. 497/

Chapter 2 

Highway Safety Administration 

Generally the cities do not have directly comparable provisions, but see the provisions discussed in the Comparison for MTO 

Article 11.498/ Chapter 3 

Certificate of Title and Registration of Vehicles 

The cities, of course, do not issue titles and most do not register vehicles. Those are primarily functions of the state governments. Nevertheless, 37 of the cities have ordinance sections which apparently duplicate some of the state law provisions on title and/or registration, or in some other way require vehicle owners and drivers to comply with those state laws so that failure to comply is a violation of a city ordinance.RR/ 

Also, eight of the 100 cities have requirements for local vehicle registration in addition to (not in lieu of) the state registration requirements.499/

Chapter 4 

Antitheft Laws 

Generally the cities do not have provisions comparable to these Uniform Vehicle Code sections. Seventeen of the cities do have very limited provisions on this topic, however. RS 

Chapter 5 

Dealers, Wreckers and Rebuilders

No city ordinances similar to these business licensing requirements have been found. 

Chapter 6 

Drivers' Licenses 

The cities do not issue drivers' licenses, but 40 of them have some driver license requirements.RT/ These provisions duplicate some of the state laws or they in some other way make the state driver licensing requirements enforceable as a city ordinance.

Chapter 7 Financial Responsibility 

Two cities, Flint, Mich. and Salt Lake City, Utah, prohibit driving in the city without complying with the state motor vehicle - 87 

---

insurance requirements. Mobile, Ala. requires a motor vehicle operated to transport persons or property for-hire over a fixed or regular route to comply with state insurance requirements. A number of cities have insurance requirements connected to the privileges given to authorized emergency vehicles in the city. 

In general, however, financial responsibility and compulsory insurance provisions are left to state laws. 

Chapter 8 

Owners of For-Rent Vehicles 

Eight cities have provisions comparable to UVC § 8-103 requiring a person in the business of renting motor vehicles to others to maintain records (name, address, etc.) of the persons renting the vehicles.500/

Chapter 9 -- Civil Liability 

Seattle, Wash. has a comparable section applicable only to violations by bicyclists. No other comparable municipal ordinances have been located.

Chapter 10 

Accidents and Accident Reports 

Sixty-five cities have some provisions on accidents and accident reports comparable to the provisions of Chapter 10 of the Uniform Vehicle Code. RU Some of these provisions have already been discussed in the Comparison for MTO SS 3-8 and 3-9.501/ Twentythree of the 65 cities have all or nearly all of the sections from the Uniform Vehicle Code in addition to (or instead of) the provisions similar to those Model Traffic Ordinance sections.RV/

Miscellaneous Provisions 

Many cities have ordinance provisions which are not drawn from either the Model Traffic Ordinance or the Uniform Vehicle 

Code and are not really comparable to the provisions of either of those documents. Some of these miscellaneous provisions are discussed in this portion of the Commentary. Some of them are discussed because they are commonly found in the ordinances of a number of the cities. Others are discussed because they are unique. Still others have been selected for discussion simply because the authors find them interesting.

Rules of the Road 

State rules adopted. Four cities have provisions which adopt the state rules of the road by reference, making them part of the city ordinances.546/

Careless driving. We have already noted the cities with pro- 547/ visions comparable to UVC § 11-901 on reckless driving.

The gravamen of that offense is operation of a vehicle in willful or wanton disregard for the safety of persons or property. In addition to, or instead of, reckless driving, 56 cities define the offense of careless driving. TQ The gravamen of this offense is generally simple negligence.548/

The language of these provisions varies considerably, but many refer to the operation of a vehicle without due caution and circumspection, or without giving full time and attention to driving, or a failure to keep the vehicle under control or to avoid a collision. The most common element is failure to avoid a collision; 16 of the provisions contain it.TR/ 

One of the 56 ordinances (Portland, Ore.) has an interesting proviso to the effect that an officer can arrest or issue a citation for careless driving only if the offense occurred in his presence. 

Cutting corners. Twenty-six cities have a provision which prohibits driving across public or private property in order to avoid compliance with a traffic-control device.TS 

Left turns. Ten cities have provisions which generally prohibit left turns into or from an alley, private road or driveway, or building or parking lot. 549 only within the central business where the restriction is signed. turns from or to a one-way street. 

Most of these restrictions apply district. Several apply only 

Most specifically exempt left 

96

Several other cities restrict left turns. Denver, Colo. prohibits left turns from a two-way street unless the turn is safe and does not interfere with other traffic. Seattle, Wash. prohibits left turns between intersections when it would delay or stop any vehicle in either direction or endanger anyone. 

Four-way stops. Three cities have right of way rules applicable at four-way stop intersections. Spokane, Wash. specifies that the first vehicle to reach the intersection has the right of way. San Antonio, Tex. and Norfolk, Va. specify likewise, but add that if two or more vehicles reach the intersection at the same time, drivers must yield to the vehicle on the right. 

Other maneuvers in traffic. Two cities have provisions relating to lane changing:  requires a driver to yield to traffic already in a lane before changing lanes. 

Albuquerque, N.M. authorizes the mayor to erect signs on roads with two or more lanes in the same direction to prohibit lane changing. One other city, Honolulu, Ha., has a provision describing traffic lane markings and their meanings. 

Three cities have provisions relating to roadway entry and exit maneuvers: Atlanta, Ga. prohibits entering or leaving a street at any point other than an intersection, alley, or private driveway. Seattle, Wash. prohibits entering a street from a driveway if a sign indicates the driveway is for entry from the street only. Seattle also prohibits backing in or out of an alley, except when guided by a qualified flagman. Toledo, Ohio prohibits backing a vehicle into an intersection or crosswalk. 

Denver, Colo. describes a rule for drivers and pedestrians when facing an inoperative or malfunctioning traffic-control signal. Essentially the driver treats it like a stop intersection; the pedestrian is required to yield and enter the roadway only when he can do so with safety.

Portland, Ore. has a provision which applies on a two-lane, two-way street. It requires the driver of a vehicle moving more slowly than the lawful speed limit to pull off the roadway at the first safe opportunity in order to allow a faster moving vehicle to pass.

St. Paul, Minn. requires the driver of a vehicle meeting or overtaking a school bus stopped on a street to receive or discharge school children to drive at a speed which is reasonable - 97 · 

---

and prudent and with due caution for the safety of the children in passing the bus. State law in Minnesota requires stopping for such a bus, however.. 550/ Two cities have provisions which describe the proper and lawful manner of waiting for a parking space which is about to be vacated. Both prohibit "stealing" a space when another driver is in the proper position to take it. The ordinance of Spokane, Wash. provides as follows:

Stealing Parking Space Prohibited. (1) No person shall willfully interfere with any driver who is in the act of lawfully parking a vehicle at the curb or at any other permissible place on the public street or highway, or steal or attempt to steal the parking place of another who has first selected it and is attempting to park a vehicle thereon. (2) All vehicles waiting for another vehicle that is moving out of a parking place, for the purpose of moving into the same, shall wait behind the vehicle that is moving out and not in front of it.551/ 

Tulsa, Okla. provides as follows:

Procedure For Occupying Vacated Parking Space. 

Every driver desiring to enter a parking space being vacated shall stop his vehicle and wait to the rear of the vacating vehicle and having so waited shall have prior right to the parking space over all other drivers. It shall be unlawful for any driver to wait in his vehicle ahead of a parking space being vacated or to attempt to interfere with one who has properly waited at the rear of a parking space being vacated. 

No driver shall stop and wait for a parking space unless the vehicle vacating the space is in the process of vacating. Every driver desiring to enter a vacant parking space shall stop his vehicle immediately in front of and parallel to said space, then back into it. It shall be unlawful for any other driver to drive his vehicle into said space, block or interfere in any way with said driver while he is preparing to back or is backing into said space.. 552/ 98. 

---

Embracing. Fourteen cities have a provision which prohibits driving while embracing another person, or with another person on the driver's lap.TT/ Several of these provisions also prohibit driving with an animal on the driver's lap. 

Passenger provisions. Salt Lake City, Utah prohibits driving a vehicle with any person standing on a seat in the vehicle. 

Four cities, Portland, Ore.; and Seattle, Spokane, and Tacoma,

Wash., prohibit carrying an animal on the exterior of a vehicle (hood, running board, fenders, or other exterior parts) unless it is in a cage or other secure enclosure. Portland also prohibits 553/ carrying a child on the exterior of a vehicle. 

Honolulu, Ha. requires a permit to carry more than five people in a truck unless they are all members of the same family. 

Evansville, Ind. prohibits discharge of passengers in a crosswalk or intersection. 

Accidents. Dayton, Ohio requires the driver of a vehicle which strikes a fire hydrant to report the fact to the police or fire chief within 24 hours.

Four cities have provisions relating to the moving of vehicles which have been involved in an accident. Cincinnati, Ohio requires moving a vehicle after a non-injury accident, if it interferes with traffic.554/ San Francisco, Cal.; and Austin, and Fort Worth, Tex. have provisions which generally prohibit moving the vehicles involved in an accident until the police arrive. Austin allows moving a vehicle to free an injured person. Fort Worth allows it to avoid a conflict or obstruction. The San Francisco ordinance applies to tow truck operators. They may move a vehicle involved in an accident only to free an injured person, and otherwise only when there are no injuries or fatalities and it is necessary to allow continued flow of streetcar, bus, or trolley traffic. 555/ 

Prohibited vehicle or driver conditions. Several cities have ordinances which describe conditions under which driving is prohibited:

Washington, D.C. prohibits driving a vehicle with the doors tied open or swinging. 

99 -

Youngstown, Ohio prohibits operating a vehicle in the "congested district" without having sufficient gas to get out of that district..

Spokane, and Tacoma, Wash. both prohibit operating a vehicle while dragging an article along the street. 

Cincinnati, Ohio prohibits vehicle operation by any person with an infirmity of mind or body which makes him incapable of safe operation.

Albuquerque, N.M. prohibits driving in weather which substantially impairs vision. It also prohibits wearing dark glasses at night.

Shreveport, La. makes it unlawful for any person to drive a motor vehicle after he has been previously convicted twice for having driven such a vehicle while in intoxicated condition. 

Provisions Relating to General Conduct 

Alcohol use. Four cities have "open-bottle laws" which prohibit having any open container of alcoholic beverage in the passenger area of a motor vehicle, and five cities make it unlawful to consume alcoholic beverages in a motor vehicle.556/ 

Eating. Tacoma, Wash. prohibits eating or drinking while operating a motor vehicle. 

Noise. Eleven cities prohibit unnecessary noise due to the manner of operation of a vehicle (such as by squealing the tires through rapid acceleration or by racing the engine), or due to the vehicle's load (a heavy cargo not "deadened," for example).

Six of the 11 refer only to operation noise, one to load noise, and four to both kinds.557/

Twenty-two cities have provisions establishing "quiet zones" around specific buildings or kinds of buildings (such as hospitals) TU

The ordinances generally prohibit any unnecessary noise or use of a horn except in an emergency in the quiet zones. 

One of the ordinances also regulates speed in the zone. Pittsburgh, designates the streets surrounding a hospital as "hospital streets, and when appropriately signed, drivers are prohibited from making unnecessary noise, and from driving at a speed "faster than a walk. "558 100.

Eight cities provide for temporary quiet zones established at the request of a physician around a building where a seriously ill person is staying.559/

Five cities have provisions in their vehicle ordinances to control the use of vehicles with loudspeakers. 560/ 

One city prohibits the use of a band or orchestra on a sidewalk or street for advertising purposes. 561/ Littering. Eight of the cities have a provision in their vehicle ordinances prohibiting littering.5627 

Literature distribution. Eight cities prohibit placing flyers, ads or other objects on a vehicle or handing (or throw- 563/ ing) such material to vehicle occupants Traffic-control devices. Several cities have provisions to protect traffic-control devices from interference. Five prohibit lights alongside the highway which interfere with the effectiveness of traffic-control signals.564/ Two cities prohibit structures or projections which conceal signs or signals. 565/ 

Two cities prohibit the unauthorized possession of official traffic-control devices.566 One of the two (Knoxville, Tenn.) authorizes the traffic engineer to indicate the ownership of signs, signals, and markers with a stamp, etching, or other permanent means.

Honolulu, Ha. prohibits tracking mud on the roadway so as to obscure roadway markings. If it is unavoidable, the responsible party is required to clean it up. Kansas City, Kans. has a related provision which prohibits a contractor or builder from permitting an accumulation of mud, substances, or debris that might be hazardous to traffic, but the section does not specifically refer to obscuring roadway markings. 

Sacramento, Cal. prohibits anyone from tampering with a traffic counting device.

Public gatherings. Four cities have provisions describing police powers to control and direct traffic at large public gatherings. San Diego, Cal. authorizes police to order vehicles or pedestrians off the streets or sidewalks, and to direct the parking of vehicles, in crowd situations. San Francisco allows police to divert traffic or restrict or prohibit parking during 

101

an emergency or a parade.

Indianapolis, Ind. allows the police or fire chief to temporarily close a street or to prohibit parking during an emergency, an emergency, or due to an accident or parade. Buffalo,

N.Y. delineates the supervisory authority of police at public gatherings.

Cincinnati, Ohio provides for the holding of "street fetes. 

The city manager, with the approval of the abutting property owners, and where it will not greatly interfere with public travel, may close a highway or part of one to vehicular traffic for the purpose of holding fetes, bazaars or similar events for religious, charitable, civic, patriotic, or fraternal purposes. 

Hartford, Conn. has a section relating to public conduct during parades. It prohibits interference with or driving through a parade, and prohibits parking on the parade route. 

Another section prohibits malicious obstruction of a street or sidewalk.

Police radio. Eight cities have provisions relating to the unauthorized interception of police radio broadcasts.567 Three of these prohibit unauthorized interception of the broadcasts; four require a license or authorization to possess a radio capable of intercepting police transmissions; one city has both provisions. 11 

One other city requires garage keepers to report any vehicle brought to their garage which is equipped with a radio capable of receiving police broadcasts.568/

Miscellaneous conduct rules. The following cities have rules as described:

St. Petersburg, Fla. makes it illegal to paint a vehicle to resemble a police or fire department vehicle. 

Riverside, Cal. prohibits leaving a child or animal unattended in a vehicle parked on a city parking lot. 

San Jose, Cal. enumerates certain activities, such as repairing autos or keeping wrecked vehicles, which are prohibited in the front or side yards of properties located in residential districts. 

Minneapolis, Minn. has a curfew provision in its vehicle code. 

It prohibits any person under the age of 17 from driving between 102 

---

the hours of 12 a.m. and 5 a.m., except in an emergency or if accompanied by an adult.

Spokane, Wash. prohibits publishing or advertising the time consumed or speed attained by a vehicle between given points if the average speed would exceed the applicable speed limit, and a conviction for violating this provision is prima facie evidence of reckless driving.569

Perhaps the most unusual provision located in this study is the last sentence of a Rochester, N.Y. section on pedestrian rights and duties. The sentence provides as follows: 

Pedestrians crossing roadways after dark must remember that motor vehicles are lighted and can be easily seen while the pedestrians do not carry a light and cannot be easily seen. 570/ 

Rules for Special Traffic Control Areas 

Off-highway traffic control. Some cities have special traffic regulations that apply in particularly described off-highway locations. Some others make all or most of their traffic ordinances applicable in some non-highway locations.571/ These provisions vary extensively, but some similarities can be described. 

Ten cities have special traffic regulations which apply within city parks.572/

These regulations encompass subjects such as restrictions on commercial vehicles, speed limits, parking prohibitions or restrictions, and animal restrictions. Only one of the 10, Memphis, Tenn., has a provision which makes all traffic ordinances generally applicable within the city parks. It should be noted with regard to all cities, however, that if roadways within parks are publicly maintained and open to public vehicular use they would be highways, and the traffic ordinances would apply thereon without need for any special provisions.

TV/ Thirty-two cities have some provision regarding traffic on city owned, non-highway property.: The property most commonly covered is city-owned parking lots or garages, but some cover other property such as airports, municipal centers, public housing projects, or the grounds of a hospital, school, or college. Some of the 32 ordinances impose special traffic regulations applicable on such city-owned property. Others impose use restrictions. 

103

Closed streets. A large number of cities have provisions relating to street closings. Thirty-two prohibit driving on any street or portion thereof which is closed by a barrier or barricade TW

Twenty-eight cities have provisions which prohibit driving over fresh paving or fresly painted roadway markings.TX/ 

The following cities have provisions relating to street closings as described:

Phoenix, Ariz. requires the city traffic engineer to develop a traffic barricade manual and to approve and keep a record of all temporary street closings.

San Francisco, Cal. prohibits erection of a barrier by a public utility unless approved by the department of public works. 

Albuquerque, N.M. sets rules for obstruction of traffic by public utility vehicles or work sites. A permit is required and conditions applicable to the roadwork site are specified. 

Syracuse, N.Y. prohibits closing a street to allow construction on private property without permission of the police chief and fire chief, except in an emergency. Where a street closing is for a public improvement, the police chief and fire chief must be given notification of the closing. 

Akron, Ohio prohibits placing an obstruction on a street without authorization.

Cincinnati, Ohio specifically authorizes the city manager to reroute traffic, to make it one-way, or to ban traffic from a street when the street is under repair or reconstruction. 

Toledo, Ohio authorizes the public safety director to temporarily close a street or divert traffic. 

Houston, Tex. requires contractors to get permission of the city engineer before erecting a barrier. The engineer is required to notify the police, fire, and public works departments. 

Traffic control around schools. Many cities have provisions relating to traffic control on streets surrounding schools. A few also have similar provisions applicable to streets surrounding playgrounds and/or churches. 

104

Twenty-one cities have provisions authorizing traffic direction by school crossing guards at crossings near schools, and requiring drivers to obey the directions.TY/ 

Warren, Mich. also provides for traffic direction by church traffic control guards near churches. 

Wichita, Kans. provides for the use of hand signs, signals, or warning devices for traffic control on streets near parks, playgrounds, or schools. Where such a device is being used to aid school children to cross the street, drivers are required to come to a complete stop and remain stationary while children are crossing the street.

Six cities have provisions relating to the closing of streets adjacent to schools during school hours. Two of these name the streets to be closed; the other four authorize the traffic engineer to close the streets.573

The Los Angeles ordinance also allows closing a street to through traffic in order to use the street for driver education.

Six cities prohibit passing within school zones during school hours. Five of the six apply the restriction in marked school zones; the sixth authorizes the engineer to create no passing zones in the vicinity of schools, theaters, and churches.574/ 

Three cities provide for school crossings or crossing zones, and regulate driving in those areas as follows: 

 provides for designation of "school stops" at certain intersections or crosswalks. When appropriate signs are displayed during limited hours, drivers must stop at the signs and yield to children and other persons crossing the road. Drivers may not proceed until it is safe. A second section contains similar provisions for "playground stops." A third section has similar provisions for "church stops. "1 Toledo, Ohio provides for designation of school crossings, and for the use of a sign at such crossings which displays the word "STOP" at times when children are proceeding to and from school, but not at other times. When the word "STOP" is displayed, all vehicles must come to a full and complete stop before entering the crosswalk, and yield to any person crossing the street. 

El Paso, Tex. provides for designation of school crossing zones by the use of signs or devices. Regulations applicable in such zones are effective during school hours. The regulations 

105 -

include no passing, speed limitation, and a requirement to come to a complete stop before the crosswalk whenever it is occupied by any person.

Provisions Relating to Bicycles 

In addition to the provisions already discussed which are comparable to sections in the Model Traffic Ordinance, a number of cities have miscellaneous ordinances concerning bicycles.575/ 

Riding in the street. Two cities have age-related restrictions on riding bicycles in the street. Indianapolis, Ind. prohibits anyone under the age of 12 from riding a bicycle on any roadway except a play street; the parent is responsible for any violation by the child. Warren, Mich. prohibits anyone under the age of 11 from riding in the street, and anyone under the age of 17 (except newspaper carriers) from riding on certain named busy streets. 

Two cities require bicyclists on the roadway to remain generally within a specified distance from the right-hand curb. Wichita, Kans. specifies five feet; Madison, Wis. specifies three feet. 

Albuquerque, N.M. requires motorists who are passing a bicycle on the road to do so only at a safe distance (not less than five feet) and speed.

Akron, Ohio requires bicyclists to come to a complete stop and make sure it is safe before entering a street from any private property. 

Two cities, New Orleans, La. and Grand Rapids, Mich., have provisions relating to bicycle accidents. They require the bicyclist to stop when involved in an accident resulting in injury or property damage, and to report to the police. 

Riding on sidewalks. Four cities have provisions regarding sidewalk riding. Two, Des Moines, Ia. and Flint, Mich., impose a speed limit (8 mph and 10 mph respectively) for bikes on the sidewalk. Indianapolis, Ind. also has a 10 mph speed limit but allows riding on sidewalks only by children under the age of 12, only when pedestrians are not using the sidewalk, and only when the child is being supervised by his parent or other responsible person. 

Fresno, Cal. requires bicyclists to dismount and walk at the junction of two sidewalks where there is an adjacent hedge or other visual obstruction. Fresno also prohibits bike riding in crosswalks. 

106 -

Riding on bike lanes. Eleven cities have provisions relating to bike lanes. Five authorize the designation of lanes on the road- 576/ way for bicycle use.

Three cities have sections which generally require bicyclists to stay within the lane when riding on streets where bike lanes have been designated.577/ Five cities regulate 578/ the direction of traffic flow on bicycle lanes. the use of bicycle lanes by other vehicular traffic. 

Five restrict 579/ 

Bicycle parking. Five cities provide for the designation of bicycle parking zones. 580/

Three cities have some other miscella- 581/ neous requirements relating to bicycle parking. 

Grand Rapids,

Mich. and Albuquerque, N.M. require all unattended bicycles to be locked.

Bicycle equipment. Albuquerque, N.M. prohibits modifying the fork length on a bicycle so that it exceeds the wheel diameter. 

Kansas City, Kans. requires all new bicycles sold to have pedal reflectors. Four cities restrict handlebar and/or pedal height for bicycles; generally they prohibit bars above shoulder height and pedals higher than 12 inches above ground at their lowest point.582/

Bicycle serial numbers. Twenty cities have a provision which prohibits alteration or destruction of the serial number on a bicycle.TZ/

Four cities require all new bikes sold to bear a serial 583/ number; the dealer is required to stamp one on if necessary..

Ten cities specify that the police may stamp a serial number on a 584 bicycle frame if the number is missing. 

Bicycle dealers. Two cities have provisions applicable to the rental of bicycles.  prohibits renting a bicycle to a minor without written parental permission. The dealer must retain the written permission for at least 24 hours. Madison, Wis. prohibits renting a bicycle to any person who is incapable of safely operating it due to alcohol, drugs, or physical or mental disability. 

Two cities have provisions applicable to sales. Dayton, Ohio prohibits buying or exchanging a bicycle from or with a minor without the parents consent. Richmond, Va. requires a dealer who acquires any used bicycle or parts for resale to report the fact to the police chief within 24 hours, and to hold the bicycle or parts for five days unless special permission to sell during that time period is obtained. 107 

---

Impounding bicycles. Eleven cities have provisions authorizing the impounding of bicycles which are abandoned or which are not registered as required, and providing for the sale or other disposition of such bicycles which are not claimed after a specified period of time.585

Twenty-three cities authorize impounding a bicycle as a penalty for traffic ordinance violations by the bicyclist.586/ 

Several of these apply only to bicycles operated by minors. 587/ Several others actually provide for suspension of the registration rather than actual impounding of the bicycle.588/ 

Examination of bicyclists. Riverside, Cal. requires all bicycles to be registered and provides as follows regarding the issuance of the registration:

No license shall be issued, as provided in this chapter, to anyone until an examination has been given wherein the applicant has demonstrated sufficient ability to safely operate a bicycle and has satisfactorily proven that he has sufficient knowledge of the traffic rules and regulations.589/

Provisions Relating to Motorcycles 

In addition to the provisions already discussed which are comparable to sections of the Uniform Vehicle Code, a few cities have miscellaneous ordinances concerning motorcycles, especially 590/ small motorcycles such as motor scooters and minibikes. lly

Nonhighway use restrictions. Eight cities prohibit or restrict the use of motorcycles and motor-driven cycles on nonhighway property. 

Generally the restrictions apply on private property. ; 

Lubbock, Tex. and Va. Beach, Va. prohibit them on private property without the owner's consent. San Jose, Cal. and Lubbock, Tex. prohibit them on public, nonhighway property. Dallas, Tex. prohibits them on all private property if without a muffler. Fresno, Cal. and 

Albuquerque, N.M. prohibit them on private property near residences, schools, and hospitals. Newport News, Va. prohibits them on school grounds. St. Louis, Mo. restricts their use in public parks. 

Highway use restrictions. Lincoln, Neb. and Oklahoma City,

Okla. prohibit minibikes on the street. Albuquerque, N.M. does the same for motorcycles with a very short wheelbase. Baltimore, Md. prohibits motorcycles or motor-driven cycles on streets unless they 

108 -

are capable of maintaining the speed limit. Detroit, Mich. prohibits the use of sports competition motorcycles on the highway. 

Miscellaneous. Detroit, Mich. requires the seller of a motordriven cycle to give notice to the purchaser that the vehicle must be registered, the driver must be licensed and at least 16 years of age, and insurance is required before the vehicle can be operated. 

Cleveland, Ohio requires a $5.00 registration fee for a motordriven bicycle if it is not required to be registered under state law.

Corpus Christi, Tex. requires the operator of a motorcycle to wear shoes.

Detroit, Mich. has a provision regulating motorcycle clubs. 

Other Special Categories of Traffic 

Mass transit and car pool. Sixteen cities have some provision relating to mass transit lanes. Twelve of the 16 provide for designation of a lane (generally the curb lane or a reverse-flow lane) 591/ as a mass transit or bus lane.. 

The other four have provisions relating to the use of such lanes, requiring their use by mass transit 592/ vehicles and/or retricting their use by other vehicles. 

Two cities, Sacramento, Cal. and Knoxville, Tenn., allow desig- 593/ nation of special parking places reserved for car pools. 

Minneapolis, Minn. has a section which is two pages long on the subject of bus "wyeing." It allows buses to wye at intersections approved by the city council, where warning signs are posted. Prior to approval of locations for wyeing, the traffic engineer is required to make an extensive study of the alternative ways for the bus to turn around at that location, such as by driving around the block, or making a U-turn. 

Handicapped drivers. Almost all of the city provisions regarding handicapped persons relate to vehicle parking. 

Provisions which grant special exemptions from parking time limits, some no parking restrictions, and parking meter fares and limits, in favor of handicapped persons are quite common in city ordinances. Twenty-four cities have one or more such exemptions for handicapped persons.UA Twelve cities grant such exemptions for disabled war veterans. 594 

109

Some cities have provisions under which some parking spaces, either on-street, off-street, or both, may be reserved by special signs for use by handicapped persons. Eleven cities have provisions of this type. 595

Concerning all of the above described parking provisions, most apply only when the handicapped person or disabled veteran is driving the vehicle being parked. Several would apply only if the vehicle owner is the handicapped person.596/ Only a few appear to apply where the handicapped person is a passenger in the vehicle, and some of those might not apply if the handicapped passenger is a child.597/ 

Minneapolis, Minn. and Oklahoma City, Okla. provide for designation of handicapped loading zones. Houston, Tex. allows disabled veterans to park in truck loading zones for up to five minutes. 

Newport News, Va. provides for reservation of a parking space in front of the home of a paraplegic. Minne- Several cities have provisions not related to parking. apolis, Minn. provides for use of a special distress flag when a handicapped person needs assistance. Minneapolis also provides for use of a whistle by a blind person needing assistance. Persons within call of the whistle are required to assist the blind person in crossing the street.  requires curb cuts appropriate for wheelchairs whenever new or replacement curbing is installed. 

Snowmobiles. Ordinances comparable to UVC § 11-1113 which 

598/ regulate snowmobile use on highways have already been noted.. 

In addition, eight cities have ordinances which regulate other aspects of snowmobile operation such as registration, equipment 5997 requirements, and minimum operator age.. 

Golf carts. San Diego, Cal. establishes two golf cart zones and specifies traffic regulations applicable in those zones. San 

Jose, Cal. prohibits electric carts on sidewalks, except when used by disabled persons. Nashville, Tenn. requires golf carts used on the highway to comply with motorcycle equipment requirements. UB Animals. Eighteen cities have provisions which relate to the use of animals, primarily horses, on highways. These provisions differ significantly from one another in scope and substance. 

Roller skates and skateboards. Fresno, Cal. prohibits roller skates and skateboards on mall streets; Long Beach, Cal. prohibits them on certain named streets. Atlanta, Ga. prohibits roller skating 

110

on sidewalks in the central business district and prohibits skaters from forming noisy crowds, clinging to vehicles, or joining hands on a sidewalk. St. Paul, Minn. prohibits roller skating in the street. Portland, Ore. prohibits skateboards on streets or sidewalks at night, and at any time in the central business district. 

Learner's permits. New York, N.Y. specifies that a person with a learner's permit may not drive in parks, on play streets, or along a block where there is a playground or park entrance. 

Portland, Ore. prohibits use of streets in the central business district for driving lessons. 

Garbage trucks. Atlanta, Ga. provides that employees and vehicles of the department of sanitary engineering while performing their duties have the right of way in the use of the streets. 

New York, N.Y. provides that garbage trucks may stand on the roadway side of a parked vehicle in order to expeditiously load trash. 

News-gathering vehicles. San Francisco, Cal. provides for issuance of a press card to newspaper, radio, and television reporters, and authorizes them to park in violation of most parking restrictions as necessary to cover a story. They may not park in tow-away zones, on exclusive transit lanes, near handicapped access ramps, or so as to obstruct emergency and firefighting operations. 

Delivery vehicles. Sacramento, Cal. and Portland, Ore. provide for issuance of delivery vehicle permits which allow certain special parking privileges while making deliveries.

Parking Provisions 

In addition to the provisions already discussed which are com parable to parking provisions in the Model Traffic Ordinance or 

Uniform Vehicle Code, many cities have parking restrictions as de- 600/ scribed below.

Parking at nonhighway locations. Twenty-eight cities prohibit UC parking on private property without permission of the owner. 

Some of these restrictions apply only if the property is posted. 

Forty cities restrict parking on city-owned property..

UD 

Parking on city streets. Provisions which prohibit parking commercial vehicles of some types on residential streets at night, or in the daytime except as necessary to load or unload, are very common. Some of the provisions apply to all streets rather than · 111

just residential streets. of this type. UE 

Seventy-seven cities have provisions

It is also common for the municipal ordinances to prohibit parking on city streets continuously for longer than a stated period of time. These are not short-term parking restrictions such as would be imposed by signs at particular locations. Rather these are provisions which generally apply to all city streets and prohibit long-term continuous parking. The time periods involved range from 12 hours to as much as five days. Some of these provisions relate to the abandoned vehicle problem and have already been discussed in that context if they create a presumption of abandonment and authorize impounding such vehicles.601 Others in the group have not been previously noted. A total of 53 cities have long-term street parking prohibitions of this type.. UF Seventeen cities have various parking restrictions intended to allow a time for street cleaning operations. UG/ 

Other specific locations. Twelve cities have provisions which generally prohibit parking on limited access highways.602/ 

Twenty-one cities prohibit parking in front of the entrances to specific kinds of public buildings such as hotels, theaters, and auditoriums. UH/ 

Seven cities prohibit parking in fire lanes.. 603/ Parking meters. Parking meter provisions comparable to those in the Model Traffic Ordinance have already been discussed.604/ 

Some cities have miscellaneous meter provisions as described below: 

Fourteen cities permit expeditious passenger or freight loading/ unloading in meter zones without paying the meter. UI Some of these are limited to freight only; some are limited to particular times of day. 

Seventeen cities specify in some manner that each period of UJ/ overtime parking in a meter zone is a separate offense.

UK Twenty-two cities have provisions relating to the temporary "hooding" of parking meters.. These ordinances all provide that a person who obtains a permit, pays a fee, and complies with certain conditions may have meters temporarily covered or otherwise taken out of service, in order to facilitate construction projects or similar temporary activities.
