---

Six cities specify parking meter fee exemptions for certain vehicles. Fresno, and Riverside, Cal. exempt monthly permit holders on certain metered lots. Mobile, Ala. exempts police and fire vehicles. Washington, D.C. and Boston, Mass. exempt city, state, and federal government vehicles; Boston adds diplomatic vehicles also.  exempts vehicles with an "official business" placard, and regulates the use of such placards. 

Baltimore, Md. provides for color coding of meter posts as an indication of the lawful time limit for parking at that meter. 

Colors are to be administratively determined. 

San Francisco, Cal. makes reference to a motorcycle parking meter zone in its ordinances. 

Parking enforcement. San Francisco, and Santa Ana, Cal. both provide that a person parking his vehicle gives implied consent to having a chalk mark placed on the tire as part of the parking enforcement program.

Columbus, Ga. provides as follows with respect to "courtesy parking tickets": (a) The police department of Columbus, Georgia is hereby authorized to issue courtesy parking tickets rather than parking violation tickets to vehicles with out-of-state tags other than Russell County and Fort Benning personnel, and Georgia tags other than Muscogee, Harris, Chattahoochee, Talbot and Marion Counties. (b) This authorization applies only to overtime parking and shall not be construed to authorize parking where it is otherwise illegal to park.605/

The named counties are those which include and surround the city of Columbus, including Russell County, Alabama. 

Business Regulation 

Taxicabs. Some cities have ordinances regulating certain aspects of the taxi business. They commonly regulate the use of taxicab zones or stands.606/ They may require drivers to remain in their cabs at stands, prohibit more than one cab from the same company at one stand, and/or specify that passengers may board the cab of their choice. Some require cab drivers to accept any passenger who is orderly and able to pay. Some regulate the manner of 

113

soliciting business; a prohibition of cruising slowly and impeding traffic while looking for business is common. Ten cities have ordinances which incorporate some or all of these provisions.607/ 

Towing services.

Fifteen cities have provisions regulating the operation of vehicle-towing services.UL Four of these merely regulate the city-contracted or city-arranged use of towing services to clear accident scenes.608/ Three others have rather extensive provisions requiring licensing of the towing business, imposing fees and insurance requirements, and controlling the operation.609/ 

The other eight cities regulate some aspects of the towing business. 

Some are fairly extensive such as Detroit, and Warren, Mich. Some are very brief, such as Oakland, and San Jose, Cal. which merely prohibit soliciting towing business at an accident scene. 

Ambulances. Five cities regulate some aspect of the ambulance business.610  requires a permit to operate any emergency vehicle, and requires the operator to keep a record of each emergency trip.

Railroads. Fifteen cities regulate some aspect of the railroad business or have one or more rules applicable to the operation of a train.UM/ Typical regulations include speed limits (generally 

6 to 10 mph) for trains passing through cities or at crossings, requirements for gates, signs, and other warning devices at crossings, right of way rules which require trains to yield at certain crossings, requirements to clear crossings to allow passage by emergency vehicles, and requirements for fencing of tracks. Restrictions on the amount of time a train may block a street have already been discussed.611/

Parking lots and garages. Ten cities have provisions applicable to the operators of private off-street parking facilities. 

Six of these are broad regulations which typically require a permit to operate such a parking facility and impose rules and standards on the operation.612, The other four are more narrow. Sacramento, Cal. merely requires parking rates to be posted. Memphis, Tenn. requires an attendant to be present if keys are left in the vehicles. 

Portland, Ore. and Dallas, Tex. require garage and lot owners to post signs to inform their patrons as to one-way restrictions on adjacent roadways, or left-turn restrictions when emerging from the lot or garage. 114

Street vendors.

Ordinances which prohibit parking for the 613/ purpose of selling goods have already been noted.. 

The following cities have provisions relating to street vendors as described: 

Tucson, Ariz. prohibits vending from the traffic side of a vehicle, and prohibits vending at one location for more than one hour continuously or more than two hours in any 24. 

Los Angeles, and Sacramento, Cal. prohibit vending on through highways, or in the central business district without a permit. 

Sacramento also prohibits vending food within 350 feet of a school, park, or playground; Baltimore, Md. prohibits the same within 300 feet of a school.

Denver, Colo. absolutely prohibits street vending of ice cream products from a vehicle. Vending of other food products from vehicles is prohibited in the central business district; at other locations the vendor must not double park, stop within 20 feet of an intersection, or otherwise park in violation of the law, and must not vend to any person standing in the street. 

Detroit, Mich. requires all ice cream vending trucks to be equipped with a stop signal arm which can be extended horizontally on the left side of the truck. The arm must bear the legend, "STOP, IF SAFE, THEN GO." The truck must also be equipped with alternately flashing lights two amber on the front; two red on the rear; and two on the stop signal arm displaying red to the rear and red and amber to the front. All of this equipment is to be actuated whenever the truck is stopped in a street to vend ice cream products. Approaching drivers must stop before reaching the truck, and then may pass at a reasonable speed not exceeding 15 mph, but must yield to pedestrians. 

Ice cream trucks must also have a convex mirror to make visible the area in front of the truck obscured by the hood. Vending ice cream is prohibited on main thoroughfares, on any streets in the central business district, on any streets where the speed limit exceeds 25 mph, and near schools on school days. Vendors may not back up to make a sale, allow unauthorized riders, vend on the street side of the truck, or vend to any person standing in the road. 

 prohibits vendors from double parking or otherwise blocking the street and requires the engine to be off while vending unless it is needed to preserve the product. The vending vehicle must have a trash receptacle and front and rear flashing lights (no color specified). Approaching vehicles must slow to five mph for 50 feet before and after passing the vendor when the flashing lights are in operation. 

115

New York, N.Y. prohibits vending from a parking meter space.

Portland, Ore. prohibits vending on a street or other public place within 200 feet of the civic auditorium or any public library, or within 200 feet of other public grounds where a concessionaire operating under a city permit or contract is selling similar goods. 

Transporters of flammables and explosives. Twelve cities provide for special routes for vehicles carrying flammable liquids or explosives 614/ Generally the vehicles are to be routed away from 615/ congested areas. Two cities prohibit such vehicles in tunnels. 

Albuquerque, N.M. prohibits leaving a vehicle unattended on any street when it contains specified dangerous substances. Knoxville, Tenn. prohibits parking on city streets at night by any vehicle used for the purpose of transporting explosives or volatile liquids, regardless of whether it is loaded or empty. Three cities prohibit parking such vehicles in proximity to dwelling houses or buildings used for public 616/ assembly, except as necessary for loading or unloading.

Special use zones. The Model Traffic Ordinance provides for designation of loading zones, passenger loading zones, bus stops and stands, and taxicab stands. Comparable city ordinances have already been discussed.617/ In addition, the following cities have interesting and different provisions for the creation of zones for particular purposes related to business or public activities: 

The El Paso, Tex. ordinance is very broad. It covers many different kinds of zones, authorizing their creation after due consideration for safety, traffic movement, and public convenience and necessity in relation to the particular location and zone. Zone types and rules for use are as follows: funeral zone, for use by mortuary vehicles at any time, and other vehicles when part of a funeral; hotel zone, for parking for up to 30 minutes to load or unload passengers or baggage; police zone, for use only by police vehicles; airport limousine zone, for use only by vehicles operating under a city concession to carry passengers to and from the airport; inspection zone, for use only by vehicles undergoing inspection for license or title purposes; mayor zone, a space as close as possible to the mayor's office for use only for his or her official car; press zone, spaces reserved for use of news reporters while on assignment; and taxicab zones, for use only by taxicabs. 

Other cities with special use zone provisions are Fresno, Cal., bank service zone, for use by persons conducting or waiting to conduct bank business; Denver, Colo., news vehicle zones, for use by reporters; 

Indianapolis, Ind., mail box zones, where parking is limited to allow access to mail boxes; Des Moines, Ia., school bus loading zone, where parking is limited on school days to allow access by school buses; and 

116 -

Austin, Tex., hospital emergency room zone, where parking is limited to 30 minutes.

Columbus, Ohio requires the owner or person in charge of a vehicle which is parked at the curb or side of a roadway, upon the request of a police officer or of the abutting property owner, to move the vehicle to allow access by a truck, trailer, or semitrailer for loading or unloading. 

Commercial drivers. Minneapolis, Minn. prohibits the employment of a person who is addicted to liquor or drugs to drive a passenger carrying vehicle. Madison, Wis. similarly prohibits employing an alcohol or controlled substance addict as a driver of a for-hire passenger vehicle. The ordinance requires discharge of any for-hire passenger vehicle driver convicted of driving while under the influence, and prohibits hiring anyone so convicted within the last six months. 

Akron, and Cleveland, Ohio regulate commercial vehicle drivers. 

They prohibit driving while ill or tired or after using any drug which affects driving ability. Drivers are required to take at least eight hours off duty after 14 hours on duty. 

Newport News, and Richmond, Va. prohibit driving a vehicle for more than 13 hours in any one 24 hour period. The provision applies to all drivers although it would most significantly affect commercial drivers.

Escort services. Dallas, Tex. regulates for-hire vehicle escort services used for traffic control in funeral processions. Conditions for licensure include insurance, and qualifications for drivers.

Highway Facilities 

Curb painting. 

Some cities, especially those in California, provide for official painting of curbs with the colors indicating stopping, standing, and parking restrictions. The Santa Ana provision is typical of those in the California cities. It provides that red curb marking prohibits stopping, standing, or parking. Yellow designates a loading zone applicable in the daytime; white is a more restrictive passenger loading zone applicable at all times of the day. Green indicates a short term parking limitation (less than 24 minutes) as indicated by a sign. Blue is used for a reserved handicapped parking space. All but two of the California cities have similar provisions.618 Phoenix, Ariz.; 

Honolulu, Ha.; and Salt Lake City, Utah have comparable curb painting provisions. All three provide that red means no parking although 

Phoenix also uses a red curb in combination with a sign to indicate a bus zone.

Phoenix and Honolulu specify that yellow designates a loading but Salt Lake City uses yellow to indicate any restriction as 

117

posted on accompanying signs. Phoenix uses green to indicate a short term parking limitation (less than 30 minutes). Honolulu and

Salt Lake City do not specify the meaning of green. 

Oakland, Cal. also provides that at the request of the property owner or occupant, the engineer may paint the curb red (no parking) for 18 inches on either side of a driveway. 

Long Beach, Cal. requires painted curb markings to bear the city emblem in order to be official markings. 

Seven cities have provisions which prohibit unauthorized painting of curbs.619/

Some of these also refer to roadway markings. One of the ordinances makes an exception for painting house numbers on the curb, but one other specifically includes house numbers in its curb painting prohibition.620/ 

Driveway curb cuts. Anaheim, Cal. requires a permit to construct a driveway in a business district and specifies conditions for issuance of such permits. Toledo, Ohio prohibits new commercial driveways which open into a safety zone or loading zone, or which are nearer than 65 feet "from an intersecting street on the stop side along any main thoroughfare." Nashville, Tenn. has very extensive provisions relating to new driveway construction. Consideration must be given to the convenience, safety, and mobility of pedestrian and vehicular traffic. No driveway may be constructed without authorization. Applicants must include complete construction plans. The ordinance contains detailed specifications as to the size and location of various kinds of driveways. Requirements for sight distance are also specified, and obstructions to traffic visibility from driveways are prohibited. 

Street name signs. Fresno, Long Beach, San Diego, and San Jose,

Cal. all require the traffic engineer to erect street name signs at all intersections where he installs traffic signal lights. 

Speed bumps. Honolulu, Ha. authorizes the transportation department to use anti-speed bumps on streets within the city. 

Criteria for location and use of such bumps are specified in the ordinance. Factors to be considered include population density in the area, especially the child population, traffic volume on the street, the width, configuration, and layout of the street, and speed limit compatibility with use of bumps. The department must find that use of the bumps will not create a hazard for residents of the street, and that there are no other or better protective devices which could be used. - 118

Poles. Atlanta, Ga. requires the traffic engineer to direct the placing of poles and wires in the streets, alleys and public places of the city so as to cause the least obstruction to public travel or to private use and enjoyment of adjacent property. The engineer is also authorized to compel the removal of superfluous poles.

Provisions Relating to Enforcement 

Responsibility of owners.

Provisions which create a presumption of vehicle owner responsibility for illegal parking of the vehicle have already been noted.621/ Several cities have provisions which to some extent make the vehicle owner responsible for violations other than illegal parking. Two cities make the owner responsible for accidents as follows: A Grand Rapids, Mich. ordinance provides that the license plates displayed on a vehicle involved in any accident will constitute a prima facie presumption that the vehicle owner was the person who operated the vehicle at the time and place where the accident occurred. Dallas, Tex. provides that the registered owner is presumed to be the driver of a vehicle involved in an accident resulting in property damage when the driver fails to stop and comply with his legal duties. Proof of vehicle ownership creates a prima facie presumption that the owner was the driver. 

A third city has a provision which may be similar but its effect is not clear. Springfield, Mass. has an ordinance providing that if a vehicle "is found upon any street or highway in violation of any provisions of these rules," and if the identity of the driver cannot be determined, the owner or person in whose name the vehicle is registered will be held prima facie responsible for the violation. It is not clear just what is included in the phrase "any provisions of these rules." The phrase might include any of the city's traffic ordinances; but due to its context, the section might also be interpreted as referring only to the requirement that the driver of a vehicle involved in an accident file a written report with the police. 

Albuquerque, N.M. has a related provision. It makes it unlawful to file a false report of license plate theft in order to avoid responsibility for a traffic citation. 

Detroit, Mich. has an ordinance providing that in proceedings for violation of the requirement to stop for a stopped school bus displaying flashing red lights, proof that the vehicle was driven in violation of the law and proof of vehicle ownership creates a presumption that the registered owner was the driver at the time of the violation. 

119

Drivers license as bail. Five cities have provisions under which the drivers license of a person arrested for a violation of the traffic ordinances may be surrendered in lieu of posting bail to secure the person's promise to appear in court to respond to the charges.622/ 

Private citizen enforcement. 

Portland, Ore. specifies that a private person may commence an action for a traffic offense provided that he certifies on the complaint before a municipal judge or clerk of court that he has reasonable grounds to believe, and does believe, that the person cited committed the offense. 

Portland also provides for payment of a reward by the city to a person providing information which leads to the arrest and conviction of a hit and run driver. The reward is $250 if the accident resulted in death, $50 if bodily injury, and $10 for a propertydamage-only accident.

SUMMARY 

1. This Commentary analyzes the traffic ordinances of the 100 largest cities in the United States. It identifies city ordinance provisions which are comparable to sections of the Model Traffic Ordinance or the Uniform Vehicle Code, as well as provisions which are not found in either of those documents. It discusses the scope of the city traffic ordinances and the authority for those ordinances. The purpose is to present a comprehensive picture of the status of municipal traffic regulation in the United States. 

2. The authority of municipalities to adopt and enforce traffic ordinances comes from state law. The extent and nature of that authority should control the scope of municipal traffic ordinances, but does not always effectively do so. 

There are two approaches commonly employed in authorizing municipal traffic ordinances. Under the first, city ordinances complement but do not duplicate state laws. State laws are enforced within the municipality; local traffic ordinances cover only those matters not covered by the state law. The Model Traffic 

Ordinance and about one-third of the study cities have this kind of authority. But the Commentary reveals that nine of these cities have ordinance sections which cover subjects which are regulated by the state code. These ordinances appear to be invalid. 

Under the second approach, city ordinances are allowed to duplicate but must not conflict with state traffic laws. Localities attempt to enforce their own ordinances instead of the 

120

same state law. Most of the study cities have this kind of authority, and most of them also have ordinances which clearly conflict with their state laws. The Commentary compares the state laws and municipal ordinances on only three of the hundreds of possible points of comparison. Even this very limited comparison revealed conflicts between the ordinances and state laws in 35 cities. These ordinances and any others which conflict with state laws would be invalid. 

3. The typical Model Traffic Ordinance provision will have a counterpart in 45 of the 100 study cities. Provisions deleted from the Model Traffic Ordinance in 1968 and thereafter also have counterparts, on average, in 45 cities. These counterpart provisions do not necessarily conform to the Model Traffic Ordinance provisions, there may be some significant differences, but they are comparable. This analysis relates to provisions which have had relatively long tenure in the Model Traffic Ordinance. Revisions and additions made within the last 15 years have a considerably lower adoption rate. 

4. The adoption rate for various MTO articles and sections varies widely. Considering articles, the most widely adopted is 

Article XXII, actually an appendix to the MTO, which deals with parking meter zones; on average, 78 cities have provisions comparable to each of the sections of this article. Other widely adopted articles (55 to 64 cities) are:

Article III Article VI 

Article IX Article XV 

Enforcement and Obedience to Traffic Regulations 

Turning Movements

Miscellaneous Driving Rules (Funeral 

Processions) 

Stopping for Loading or Unloading Only

The least adopted article is Article XX -- Effect and Short Title of Ordinance; only 23 cities, on average, have these provisions. 

Other poorly adopted articles (29 to 33 cities) are:

Article II Article XII 

Traffic Administration 

Regulations for Bicycles

There are seven MTO sections which have counterparts in 80 or more cities. The most widely adopted provision is MTO § 22-4 which requires deposit of coins in a parking meter; 92 of the 100 cities have this section. The other six widely adopted sections are: 

121 -

MTO S 1 MTO S 14-4 MTOS 15-4 MTO S 19-13 MTO S 22-2 MTO S 22-6 

Definition of words and phrases Parking for certain purposes prohibited Standing in loading zone 

Impounding vehicles 

Installation of parking meters 

Tampering with meters

In addition, two former sections of the Model Traffic Ordinance each have counterparts in the ordinances of 80 cities: Former MTO § 3-3 required obedience to police and fire officials directing traffic. 

Former MTO § 14-1 prohibited stopping, standing, and parking at various locations.

On the other hand, the following Model Traffic Ordinance sections have been adopted by 20 or fewer cities although all have been in the Ordinance since at least 1956:

MTO § 2-12 Traffic commission

Publication of ordinances 5. 

In 1971, the Model Traffic Ordinance was extensively amended to augment the authority of the city traffic engineer. The engineer was authorized, on the basis of an engineering and traffic investigation, to do the following at particular locations: establish many speed limits; designate one-way streets; designate through streets, and stop and yield intersections; declare prohibitions or limitations on stopping, standing, and parking; prohibit certain classes of traffic based on vehicle size or weight, or due to incompatibility with other traffic; and designate parking meter zones. All of these determinations had previously been made by the city council. The purpose of giving this authority to the engineer was to take advantage of his training and expertise and to insulate these decisions from undue political influence. 

Relatively few cities give their traffic engineer the kind of broad authority to regulate traffic which is found in the Model Traffic Ordinance. The majority of the cities leave most of this authority with the city council; generally, the engineers responsibility is to make recommendations to the council and to install traffic-control devices necessary to implement the council's decisions. 

· 122 -

6.

The Commentary also compares the city ordinances with the 

Uniform Vehicle Code. Many Uniform Vehicle Code subjects such as driver licensing, vehicle registration, equipment requirements, and size/weight restrictions which have never been covered in the Model Traffic Ordinance are covered by the municipal ordinances. 

One-third to one-half of the study cities have provisions of this type. 7.

Two-thirds of the study cities have a signal legend ordinance. These ordinances describe the meaning of the red, yellow, and green lights used in traffic-control signals. A signal legend law was deleted from the Model Traffic Ordinance in 1968 because the Uniform Vehicle Code contains such a provision. Of the cities which have signal legend provisions, 34 permit a right turn on red unless a sign prohibits it, 16 permit a right turn on red only where a sign allows it, and 18 do not specifically permit a right turn against a red light. 

8. The general approach of the Model Traffic Ordinance to speed regulation is that the state speed limit laws are applicable on streets within the city except where the city traffic engineer has placed signs to alter that limit. Only 20 cities make state speed limits applicable in the city except where altered by city officials, and only three of those 20 allow the traffic engineer to make those alterations. In lieu of following the Model Traffic 

Ordinance approach, most cities have municipal speed limits set out in their ordinances. 

9. The ordinances of 50 cities provide for absolute speed limits; it is a violation to exceed such speed limits. The ordinances of 35 other cities provide for prima facie speed limits; exceeding a prima facie speed limit merely creates a presumption that the driver was exceeding a reasonable and safe speed; a violation of the basic speed rule. Prima facie speed limits are effective and enforceable only in the context of the basic speed rule. 

But 14 of the 35 cities with prima facie limits do not include the basic rule in their ordinances. 

10. The Model Traffic Ordinance and 60 of the study cities have provisions which restrict the locations where U-turns can be made. Although there are some common elements, there is very little uniformity. The most common prohibition is against all 

U-turns unless they can be made with safety and without interfering with other traffic; two-thirds of the 60 provisions include that. 

Half of the provisions include the Model Traffic Ordinance prohibi tion of U-turns in a business district.

Some others prohibit U-turns 

123

only in the central business district. One-fourth of the provisions prohibit U-turns on curves and hill crests where visibility is limited. 

Almost all of these ordinances include other restrictions on U-turns, however, and many of them are unique. One interesting aspect of the problem involves intersections. One-third of the 60 sections prohibit U-turns at all intersections or all signalized intersections.

But a dozen cities specify in some way that intersections are the only lawful places for making U-turns. 

Among the 11. Another area of nonuniformity involves impounding of vehicles. The Model Traffic Ordinance authorizes impoundment of vehicles of scofflaws, and of any vehicle when authorized by state law. Ninety cities have vehicle impoundment ordinances. many grounds for impoundment listed in these ordinances are the following: when necessary to prevent a traffic obstruction; when the vehicle is illegally parked; when the vehicle has been reported stolen; when the driver has been arrested; when the vehicle has been involved in an accident; when the vehicle has been left continuously on the street for a long period of time; when the vehicle is abandoned; when necessary for public convenience or safety; when necessary for snow removal or street cleaning; when the vehicle is not in proper operating condition; when the vehicle has been involved in a hit and run accident or other crime; when the vehicle or its contents are needed for evidence; and the vehicle of a scofflaw. There are many variations in the precise requirements for each of these grounds for impoundment. Also, the ordinances differ substantially with regard to the procedures described for impoundment and owner notification. 

12. The Commentary also reviews nearly 200 provisions which are not found in either the Model Traffic Ordinance or the Uniform Vehicle Code, but which are found in one or more of the study cities. 

These provisions cover many different subject areas such as rules of the road, rules of general conduct, rules applicable in special areas, provisions applicable to special categories of traffic such as bicycles, motorcycles, handicapped persons, animals, etc.; parking restrictions, business regulations, and provisions relating to the streets and highways. 13.

The Commentary recommends state law revisions to narrow the authorized scope of municipal traffic ordinances so that they complement the state vehicle code but do not deal with subjects addressed by that code. It also recommends that each state maintain a state model traffic ordinance for use, including adoption by reference, by cities in that state. 

124

OBSERVATIONS AND RECOMMENDATIONS Regarding the Scope of Municipal Traffic Regulation 

The problem. In the Introduction to the Commentary, we promised a broad assessment of the scope of municipal traffic regulation. Here it is. Most of the 100 cities covered by this study have ordinances which cover far too many subjects. In addi- tion to dealing with their unique municipal traffic problems, the cities attempt to duplicate the scope of their state vehicle codes; they try to make every violation of the state code also a violation of the city ordinance. Such duplication is unnecessary and very undesirable.

Resulting conflicts. One result of such duplication is conflict between state laws and municipal ordinances. This occurs when the cities fail to keep up with changes made in the state laws by the legislature. Ordinances which conflict with state laws are null and void. But often the conflict is subtle; the ordinance differs somewhat from the state law, but it is not clear that it is a conflict until a court declares the ordinance void. meantime, the difference can create confusion as to the precise substance of the law, and there is potential for considerable intrastate nonuniformity of traffic regulation as each city ignores the state law in favor of its own ordinance on the same subject. 

In the

Our study of the 100 city ordinances leads us to conclude that this problem is widespread. We specifically examined only three substantive points, comparing the municipal ordinances with the state laws on the same points. We found 63 examples of differences which we believe would constitute conflicts involving the ordinances of 35 of the study cities. This is a rather extraordinary number of conflicts to find in such a limited comparison. We believe that a complete comparison of municipal ordinances with the state laws which they purport to duplicate would reveal that wherever cities are allowed to have ordinances on the same subjects as those covered by state law, serious conflicts will exist. 

Inefficiency. One can hardly conceive of a more complicated, confusing, and expensive system of traffic regulation than having a comprehensive state traffic law which is then duplicated, sectionby-section, by every municipality in the state. If a state has eighty cities with municipal traffic ordinances, each section of the state traffic law will have to be reproduced as a proposed ordinance for eighty city councils. Then, after being enacted, each 

125

section will again have to be reproduced in eighty city codes. 

Each year, someone in each of the eighty cities will have to study state legislation to pick up any traffic law revisions. Those revisions will have to be reproduced eighty times as proposed ordinances, and again eighty times to incorporate them into the city codes. If the cities fail to do this each year, their ordinances will then fall out of conformity, with the state law, probably making them void. Yet this is precisely the system which is authorized (though not required) by most state codes, and it is precisely this system which most of the study cities attempt to use. 

Revenue considerations. Why do so many cities attempt to maintain ordinances which duplicate state traffic laws? The answer appears to be revenue. Fines for violations of local ordinances are retained by the city, while fines for violation of the state law must be forwarded to the state government. Wherever this method of fine disposition is utilized, the cities have a powerful incentive to try to maintain ordinances which duplicate every state traffic law. 

But it is not necessary that fines be distributed in that manner. 

State law can specify any desired distribution of that revenue; the disposition need not depend on whether it was a state law or a local ordinance which was violated. 

Conclusions. We believe that this attempt by many cities to maintain ordinances which duplicate the state laws is the most serious problem in the field of municipal traffic regulation. It invariably results in conflicts between the ordinances and the laws; it leads to confusion regarding the substance of traffic regulation, and it creates potential for serious, intrastate nonuniformity. It also adds considerable bulk to the traffic ordinances and makes it more difficult for each city to maintain a traffic code which is substantively sound, up to date, well organized and useful, and generally available for those who need to use it. 

Recommendations. The solution to the problem lies more in state law than in municipal ordinance. We strongly recommend that state laws be amended, where necessary, in the following two ways: 

First, each state should have a law providing for an equitable distribution of fine revenue between state and local government. 

The method of distribution of fine revenue should not serve as an incentive to the cities to maintain ordinances which cover subjects already covered by the state law. We recommend UVC § 17-401 (Supp. 

1979) as a model provision for disposition of fines and forfeitures. 

For further discussion and the text of this section, see page 6 and footnote 12/. 

126 -

Second, each state should have a law which specifies that the state vehicle code is applicable throughout the state and in all political subdivisions and municipalities, and which specifically prohibits any local ordinances covering any subject which is covered by the state law. Municipal traffic ordinances should deal only with subjects which are not covered by the state vehicle code. We recommend UVC § 15-101 (1968, Supp. 1979) as a model for the state law. For further discussion and the text of this section, see page 5.

Regarding the Need for State Models 

Role of the Model Traffic Ordinance. In this study, we have directly compared the city traffic ordinances with the Model Traffic Ordinance (MTO). We have done so in order to conveniently present a comprehensive picture of the status of municipal traffic regulation. But we are not suggesting that any city should take the MTO and revise its traffic ordinances to conform with it. The MTO is consistent with the Uniform Vehicle Code, but it is not necessarily consistent with the laws of any particular state, and consistency between municipal ordinances and the laws of the state is absolutely essential. Any city using the MTO directly as a model for its own traffic ordinances will need to carefully analyze its state traffic laws to assure that the ordinance is consistent with the state law. 

State model traffic ordinances. The MTO is intended to be used primarily at the state level as a guide in the preparation of a model traffic ordinance for each state. Each state model traffic ordinance should be formulated so as to be entirely consistent with the laws of the particular state; the MTO should be used as a guide in this process. Then, the state model traffic ordinance can be used by each local government in that state as it considers its own traffic ordinances. 

Approximately one-third of the states have state model traffic ordinances. Most of these are developed and published by private agencies such as the state league of cities or association of mayors. 

A few of the state models are produced by state agencies such as the department of transportation or state police. One state, Missouri, has adopted a model municipal traffic ordinance as part of its state code (Mo. Ann. Stat. $$ 300.010 to 300.525).

Ordinance codification and publication problems. The results of our attempt to obtain the traffic ordinances of the 100 largest cities suggest that codification and publication of the traffic 

127.

ordinances is a serious problem for many of the cities. Although most of the ordinances were codified, few were completely up to date in the codification process; almost all supplemented their codes with uncodified ordinances adopted over the last few years. 

Most of the cities do not have copies of their code available for sale; most had to make photocopies of their codes, billing us on a per page basis. 

We believe that municipal traffic ordinances should be codified, well organized, and appropriately indexed, and the codification should be updated regularly to incorporate revisions. It should be generally available in a convenient format for use by traffic engineers, police officers, traffic court personnel and defense lawyers, and the general public. On the basis of the ordinances we received for this study, we would conclude that most of the 100 largest cities do not meet these criteria for publication of their ordinances.

One very significant advantage of a state model traffic ordinance is that it can be adopted by reference as a city ordinance. 

The state model can be printed in sufficient numbers to justify the production costs, and it can be revised annually to incorporate changes. Cities can adopt the model by reference, doing so each year in order to keep their ordinances up to date. They can then obtain sufficient copies to meet their needs inexpensively. This allows even the smallest city to have a sound traffic code without bearing the substantial expense of codification and publication. 

Conclusions. We believe that a well developed and properly maintained state model traffic ordinance is a necessary element to an effective program of municipal traffic regulation. Such a model promotes intrastate uniformity by offering the municipalities · sound traffic regulations which are consistent with state law. 

Such a model will also greatly ease the burden of the cities in developing, codifying, and publishing their traffic ordinances. 

It will allow the cities to combine their efforts so that one agency can perform the work now being done independently in hundreds of cities.

Recommendations. Again, the solutions to the problems addressed here seem to lie at the state level. We strongly recommend that each state develop and maintain a state model traffic ordinance which the cities in the state can adopt by reference or use as a guide for their own traffic code. This recommendation involves the following two elements: 

128

First, no city can have a substantively sound traffic code if the state vehicle code is deficient. The first step in developing an effective program of municipal traffic regulation is to revise the state vehicle code as necessary so that it contains the best possible provisions to promote safe and efficient use of the highways. We recommend that each state evaluate its vehicle code, especially the rules of the road, by comparing it to the Uniform Vehicle Code. A very helpful tool for performing such an evaluation is Rules of the Road Rated,* a numerical rating of the rules of the road for each state which is prepared annually by the staff of the National Committee on Uniform Traffic Laws and Ordinances. 

This document shows very quickly where the state rules of the road differ substantially from the Uniform Vehicle Code, or where the state lacks a provision comparable to one in the Uniform Vehicle Code.

Second, an agency or organization should be designated to develop and maintain on a regular and continuing basis a state model traffic ordinance. In developing the model, the MTO should serve as a guide and standard, but it should give way where necessary to maintain consistency with the state law. The state model should be revised annually to reflect revisions made in the state code and as needed to incorporate new concepts. The state model should be published in a convenient format which can be utilized as the traffic code of any city which chooses to adopt the model by reference.

154/ The three are San Francisco, Cal.; Portland, Ore.; and Virginia

Beach, Va. The San Francisco and Portland ordinances broadly ban such toy vehicles from both roadways and sidewalks within business districts or within a defined area of the city. In addition to their provisions like MTO § 3-5, San Diego, Cal. and Atlanta, Ga. have similar broad bans. The Virginia Beach ordinance is unusual. 

It prohibits playing or using toy vehicles or devices on the highway, except on sidewalks, but allows portions of streets to be designated for play. Where such designations are made on a two-lane street, persons playing or using toy vehicles in the street must keep as near as reasonably possible to the left side, facing oncoming traffic at all times.

208 These six cities are all the Ohio cities except Cincinnati.

They adopt the language of the Ohio state speed laws as their local limits, and provide for alteration of these limits by the state department of transportation, at the request of the city. 

The cities do have authority to increase the limits on through highways, or on highways where intersections are widely spaced, provided the highway is not part of the state system. Akron gives this authority to the city traffic engineer. The other five cities apparently require action by the governing body. All six require an engineering and traffic investigation.

249/ The three which refer to any intersection are Detroit and Warren, Mich., and Salt Lake City, Utah (unless permitted by a sign). 

The four are Washington, D.C.; and Austin, Fort Worth, and 

Houston, Tex. The Washington law includes intersections controlled by signal lights or a police officer, and it also prohibits U-turns on the crosswalks adjacent to such intersections. The Fort Worth and Houston ordinances allow the turns if a sign at the intersection permits them.

The Salt Lake City ordinance seems to provide that on a road with a marked center line, a U-turn can only be made at an intersection where permitted by signs. On streets without such a marked center line, U-turns appear to be lawful only when made at least 100 feet away from any intersection. In relevant part, the ordi nance specifies as follows: 

Sec. 196. Limitations on U-turns. (1) The driver of any vehicle shall not make a U-turn at any intersection unless permitted by sign or between intersections on any street with a marked center line, or within 100 feet of any intersection on any street without a marked center line.

385/ Worcester, Mass.; Nashville, Tenn.; and Tacoma, Wash. all specify 12 feet. Flint, Mich. specifies 16 feet. Chicago, Ill. requires 15 feet on a two-way street, and 10 feet on a one-way street. 

161

Five cities specify clearance distances between the vehicle and the centerline of the roadway. Omaha, Neb. and Charlotte, N.C. both have provisions conforming to MTO § 14-1. Omaha adds a prohibition against parking within five feet of the centerline on a roadway with width over 30 feet. Charlotte additionally requires 10 feet of clearance between the vehicle and the centerline, on any roadway with marked lanes. Austin, Tex. requires 10 feet of roadway clearance on roadways less than 34 feet wide, and 10 feet of clearance to the centerline on roadways over 34 feet wide. 

Sacramento, Cal. and Lubbock, Tex. have the following restrictions instead of a provision like MTO § 14-1: Sacramento requires 12 

feet of clearance between the vehicle and the centerline on any roadway without curbs. Lubbock requires 10 feet of clearance between the parked vehicle and the centerline.

447/ UVC § 15-112(c) (Supp. 1979) provides that any police officer who has reasonable grounds to believe that a motor vehicle, trailer, or semitrailer has been abandoned may remove it or cause it to be removed at the owner's expense to the nearest garage or other place of safety. 

Any police officer is hereby authorized to remove or cause to be removed to a place of safety any unattended vehicle illegally left standing upon any highway, bridge, causeway, or in any tunnel, in such position or under such circumstances as to obstruct the normal movement of traffic. 

449/ UVC S 11-1003 (c) (1968) provides as follows: 

Any police officer is hereby authorized to remove or cause to be removed to the nearest garage or other place of safety any vehicle found upon a highway when: 

1. Report has been made that such vehicle has been stolen or taken without the consent of its owner, or 2. The person or persons in charge of such vehicle are unable to provide for its custody or removal, or 3. When the person driving or in control of such vehicle is arrested for an alleged offense for which the officer is required by law to take the person arrested before a proper magistrate without unnecessary delay. 

450 MTO S 19-6(a) (1962) provided as follows: 

Members of the police department are hereby authorized to remove a vehicle from a street or highway to the nearest garage or other place of safety, or to a garage designated or maintained by the police department, or otherwise maintained by this city under the circumstances hereinafter enumerated. 

167

1. When any vehicle is left unattended upon any bridge, viaduct, or causeway, or in any tube or tunnel where such vehicle constitutes an obstruction to traffic. 

2. When a vehicle upon a highway is so disabled as to constitute an obstruction to traffic and the person or persons in charge of the vehicle are by reason of physical injury incapacitated to such an extent as to be unable to provide for its custody or removal. 

3. When any vehicle is left unattended upon a street and is so parked illegally as to constitute a definite hazard or obstruction to the normal movement of traffic.

Cincinnati, and Youngstown, Ohio; Knoxville, Memphis, and Nashville, Tenn.; San Antonio, Tex,; and Norvolk, Va. The Cincinnati and

Youngstown provisions do not specifically require a traffic obstruction; Cincinnati requires merely that the vehicle "has been involved in an accident; " Youngstown specifies that the vehicle is in stalled or damaged condition on the public streets. The Warren ordinance specifically allows the owner or operator to provide for removal of the vehicle by a towing service of his own choice. 

455/ Kansas City, and Wichita, Kans. allow impoundment when the person in charge of the vehicle is "unable to provide for its custody or removal." Flint, Mich. allows impoundment when the operator of the vehicle appears to be unfit to operate with safety. 

Dayton, Ohio allows impoundment when the person in charge of the vehicle is physically incapacitated.

458/ Six of these cities allow impounding a vehicle which is suspected to have been involved in a hit and run accident or in a felony crime. The six are Denver, Colo.; ; 

Charlotte, N.C.; and Akron, Cleveland, and Columbus, Ohio. others, Portland, Ore.; and Seattle, and Spokane, Wash. allow impounding if the vehicle or its contents are needed as evidence. Three

Seattle and Spokane also authorize impounding any vehicle used in violation of the traffic code (apparently any violation) or used so as to endanger another highway user. Albuquerque, N.M. allows impounding any vehicle containing a dangerous substance such as an explosive or flammable substance. 

Denver, Colo. and Akron, Cleveland, and Columbus, Ohio allow impounding a vehicle driven by a person without a valid drivers license. Lubbock, Tex. has a similar provision applicable only to a juvenile, unlicensed driver.

Portland, Ore. and Miami, Fla. specifically authorize impounding a vehicle illegally parked in a handicapped parking space. 

Denver, Colo. allows impounding a motor vehicle left on the street with the engine running or the key in the ignition, and any vehicle obstructing railroad tracks. 

Sacramento, and San Jose, Cal. and Albuquerque, N.M. allow impounding a vehicle in order to clear a street for a special use such as a parade, heavy traffic flow, movement of a large vehicle, etc., provided that the street is signed to prohibit parking at least 24 hours in advance. Albuquerque also allows impounding a vehicle which is obstructing work by a public utility, but the impounding expenses are paid by the utility rather than the vehicle owner. 

459/ The 29 cities listed in tabular footnote QF have ordinances of this type, and many of them declare the vehicle to constitute a nuisance. 

460 Greensboro, N.C., for example, has a provision declaring that ten designated sections which prohibit parking in described locations are adopted for the protection of public health, to afford maximum fire protection, and to alleviate dangerous traffic congestion. Vehicles parked in violation of these ten sections may be towed. 169 

---

Dayton, Ohio has a provision declaring the purpose of impoundment to be, [A]n enforcement procedure for protection of the public peace, safety, and welfare, and the safeguarding of property, and shall be used generally for the prevention and removal of traffic hazards, prevention and abatement of public nuisances arising from traffic law violations, protection of the public rights in the use of city streets, highways and roadways from obstructions placed and left in derogation of those rights, and for safeguarding and protecting recovered stolen vehicles. 

Oklahoma City, Okla. has a very similar provision. 

Omaha, Neb. allows impoundment of a vehicle obstructing traffic or illegally parked only if it is found to endanger the public health and safety.

554 The Cincinnati provision is as follows: 

$ 508-5. Vehicle Obstructing After Collision. 

Whenever, following a collision not involving personal injuries, a vehicle, whether or not the same is damaged or wrecked, is so placed as to interfere with traffic or with the proper operation and service of the divisions of fire, police, or highway maintenance, or with the operation of motor buses, the driver or other person in charge of such vehicle shall, without delay, move or cause the same to be moved to a location where it will not interfere with or impede traffic operations.

Whenever any such vehicle is left unattended, or whenever, upon request, the owner or operator of the same fails to remove it as hereinbefore provided, any member of the police force or fire force, or employee of the division of highway maintenance or of a transit company affected by such vehicle shall be authorized to remove it to a location where it will not interfere with or impede traffic operations. 

555/ The San Francisco provision is as follows: 

Sec. 24. Driver of Wreckers' Trucks Not To Interfere With Police Investigation of Accidents. 

In the case of any collision or accident of one or more vehicles on any street or in any public place within the City and County of San Francisco, where 

176

the police have been summoned for the purpose of conducting an investigation, it shall be unlawful for the driver, or any person in charge of any vehicle with a crane, hoist, winch, or towing device, who has answered a call to, or is attending the scene of such accident, to remove to remove or attempt to remove any vehicle involved in such collision or accident, or in any way to interfere with or change the position of any such vehicle, except upon authorization or direction and in the presence of a police officer, unless such change is made or attempted for the purpose of releasing a person or persons imprisoned within or under any such vehicle involved in such collision or accident, except that removal to the side of the roadway is authorized in accidents involving property damage only, when necessary to allow for the continued flow of street car, bus and trolley coach traffic.

569/ This rather unusual provision from Spokane reads as follows: 

46.61.535 Advertising of Unlawful Speed Attained,

Reckless Driving. It shall be unlawful for any manufacturer, dealer, distributor or any person, firm or corporation to publish or advertise or offer for publication or advertisement, or to consent or cause to be published or advertised the time consumed or speed attained by a vehicle between given points or over given or designated distances upon any public street or highway of this city when such published or advertised time consumed or speed attained shall indicate an average rate of speed between given points or over given or designated distance in excess of the maximum rate of speed allowed between such points or at a rate of speed which would constitute reckless driving between such points. Conviction for a violation of any of the provisions of this section shall be prima facie evidence of reckless driving and shall subject such person, firm or corporation to the penalties in such cases provided. 

The purpose of this section is not clear. The purpose and effect of the last sentence, which creates a prima facie presumption on the basis of the conviction, is particularly unclear.

571/ As to where traffic ordinances generally apply, see the discussion in the Comparison for MTO S 20-1, supra. In the absence of a provision comparable to MTO § 20-1 which limits the application of the traffic ordinances to the highways, the extent of application of the ordinances is unclear. It is possible they would apply at all locations in the city, both on and off the highways.

573/ Long Beach, Cal. and Bridgeport, Conn. name the streets to be closed. Los Angeles, Cal.; ; ; and Norfolk, Va. authorize closures. The Los Angeles provision allows closing a street only if it crosses or divides school property; when closed, no vehicular traffic is permitted. St. Louis, Jersey City, and Norfolk close the streets only to through traffic; they allow vehicular traffic to and from the school or to and from residences or businesses on the closed streets. 

574/ Indianapolis, Ind. authorizes creation of no passing zones. 

The other five cities are Wichita, Kans.; Albuquerque, N.M.; and 

Corpus Christi, El Paso, and San Antonio, Tex. Corpus Christi allows passing in such zones on streets with marked traffic lanes. 

San Antonio allows passing only a transit bus at a bus stop within such zones.

593/ The Knoxville, Tenn. ordinance (§ 26-1335) provides as follows: 

A. The Department of Traffic Engineering is hereby authorized to designate by the installation of appropriate signs parking spaces for the exclusive use of shared ride vehicles. Such reserved parking spaces shall be established only where: 

1. A significant number of registered shared ride vehicles regularly park in the area; and 2. 3. 4. 

No off-street parking facilities are available or such facilities as are available are grossly inadequate, and

Such reserved parking zone is in the interest of public convenience and welfare, and 

The Director of Public Transportation Services has certified compliance with the previously cited criteria and requested such a designation. 

In no event shall more than one-half of the total number of parking spaces in any one block be reserved for shared ride vehicles. Where official sign (s) are erected designating reserved parking spaces for shared ride vehicles, no person shall park a vehicle upon any street or portion thereof so signed unless such vehicle prominently displays a unique certificate issued by the Department of Public Transportation Services.

---

597/ Several of the ordinances exempt a vehicle which is parked by the handicapped person "or under his direction and for his use." 

See, for example, Tucson, Ariz.; Minneapolis, and St. Paul, Minn.

These would appear to cover a passenger but might not cover a child. 

Cleveland, Ohio refers to a vehicle "being operated by or for the transport of a handicapped person. The Omaha, Neb. (§35.06.115, in part) section provides as follows: 

The Police Division of the Public Safety Department of the City of Omaha is hereby authorized to issue a permit to any and all visually and physically handicapped persons who have reached school age, which shall entitle the holder thereof to free parking. For the purpose of this Section, a physically handicapped person shall mean those physically handicapped persons using crutches, canes, walkers, wheelchairs, or having definite walking problems, including but not limited to amputees, and those persons who have respiratory problems which incapacitate their walking. Visually handicapped persons qualify by the trained use of the cane or seeing eye dog.
